55	\maketitle
56	
57	\begin{abstract}
58	Pioneering time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT, TimesFM, and TTM pair a frozen backbone with a task-specific head. Because downstream series mix heterogeneous regimes, the natural upgrade is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) head: a router over architecturally distinct experts, one per window. However, this upgrade fails on instance-normalized backbones: Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN) and similar layers strip the per-window mean and variance the router needs, a failure we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}. Whenever joint training makes even one backbone transformer block trainable, routing entropy falls to $0.000$, meaning that every input is sent to a single expert and the rest are never activated. A $720$-run sweep across standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, expert-choice) recovers at most $10.9\%$ MSE ($2.7\times$ worse than our causal intervention), because the failure is in the router's input, not its optimization. We formalize this via a mutual-information decomposition; the resulting signal-ratio statistic, computable before training, predicts dataset vulnerability (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$) and flags boundary cases. The diagnosis prescribes a minimal causal intervention: \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA sets a new SOTA for adapter-based TSFM transfer under a strictly frozen backbone: $54/54$ wins on the primary backbone ($26$--$79\%$ MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter), strictly beats LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning at $p{<}0.001$, and generalizes across four further backbones and an imputation task. Eight causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and predict a \emph{Frozen Paradox}: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$, dominated by the MoE pool's architectural advantage with a smaller within-architecture freeze effect ($\leq 13\%$) attributable to gradient co-adaptation (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}). Applying the same raw-signal principle at the expert level (Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}) closes the gap with DLinear on $107/123$ cells across six backbones $\times$ four horizons.
59	\end{abstract}
60	
61	\section{Introduction}
62	\label{sec:intro}
63	
64	Time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, and Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} now provide pretrained representations for time series, alongside further pretrained variants~\citep{lee2024units, rasul2024lagllama, ekambaram2024ttm}, with some recent designs pushing toward billion-parameter scales~\citep{shi2024timemoe, liu2026timers1}. Using one of these backbones on a downstream task still requires attaching a lightweight adapter head that maps hidden states to predictions; this paper studies the adapter, holding the backbone fixed.

## Audit against supervisor's 3 rules

**Current abstract: ~290 words, 1 paragraph.** Has problem + approach + results, but violates "no detailed technical descriptions" and "no abbreviations" in 6 places.

### Violations

**A. Non-obvious abbreviations introduced just to be defined:**
- `TSFMs`, `RevIN`, `RR-MoA`, `TTM` — all defined inline. `TTM` and `RevIN` are obscure outside the niche; `TSFM` adds nothing the spelled-out form doesn't.
- `SOTA` — informal jargon; spell out.
- `MSE`, `MoE`, `LoRA` — borderline-obvious in ML; defensible.

**B. Detailed technical descriptions that belong in the body:**
1. *Opening name-drop* — "MOMENT, TimesFM, and TTM pair a frozen backbone with a task-specific head" → background, not problem.
2. *Rescue mechanism list* — "(load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, expert-choice)" → enumerates §G.
3. *Statistic spec* — "(Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$)" → method-section number.
4. *Mechanism decomposition* — "dominated by the MoE pool's architectural advantage with a smaller within-architecture freeze effect ($\leq 13\%$) attributable to gradient co-adaptation" → §3 result, not a headline.
5. *Internal cross-ref* — `(Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen})` → reader of an abstract cannot follow this.
6. *Grid specs* — "$54/54$ wins on the primary backbone", "$107/123$ cells across six backbones $\times$ four horizons", "Eight causal controls" → counts that should compress.

### Minimal-edit proposal

Five surgical cuts; preserves every claim, removes ~70 words, fixes both rule classes.

| # | Cut | Replace with |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | "Pioneering time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT, TimesFM, and TTM pair a frozen backbone with a task-specific head." | "Time series foundation models pair a frozen pretrained backbone with a task-specific head." |
| 2 | "(load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, expert-choice)" | *(delete parenthetical)* |
| 3 | "(Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$)" | *(delete parenthetical)* |
| 4 | "Eight causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and predict a *Frozen Paradox*: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$–$79\%$, dominated by the MoE pool's architectural advantage with a smaller within-architecture freeze effect ($\leq 13\%$) attributable to gradient co-adaptation (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen})." | "Causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and reveal a *Frozen Paradox*: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$–$79\%$." |
| 5 | "$107/123$ cells across six backbones $\times$ four horizons" | "the strong DLinear baseline across six backbones and four horizons" |

Optional further trim: "sets a new SOTA" → "sets a new state of the art".

### Result

Lands at ~220 words, single paragraph. Reads as: **problem** (MoE-on-instance-normalized backbones collapses) → **approach** (route on the raw input; signal-ratio diagnostic) → **results** (54/54 wins, beats LoRA/TRACE/AdaMix/full FT, generalizes, Frozen Paradox, closes DLinear gap). No internal references, no mechanism enumerations, no statistic specs.

the supervisosr insinsted on following this structure: "Abstract:
Although pre-trained transformers and reprogrammed text-based LLMs have shown strong performance on time series tasks, the best-performing architectures vary widely across tasks, with most models narrowly focused on specific areas, such as time series forecasting. Unifying predictive and generative time series tasks within a single model remains challenging. We introduce UniTS, a unified multi-task time series model that utilizes task tokenization to integrate predictive and generative tasks into a single framework. UniTS employs a modified transformer block to capture universal time series representations, enabling transferability from a heterogeneous, multi-domain pre-training dataset—characterized by diverse dynamic patterns, sampling rates, and temporal scales—to a wide range of downstream datasets with varied task specifications and data domains. Tested on 38 datasets across human activity sensors, healthcare, engineering, and finance, UniTS achieves superior performance compared to 12 forecasting models, 20 classification models, 18 anomaly detection models, and 16 imputation models, including adapted text-based LLMs. UniTS also demonstrates strong few-shot and prompt capabilities when applied to new domains and tasks. In single-task settings, UniTS outperforms competitive task-specialized time series models. Code and datasets are available at https://github.com/mims-harvard/UniTS." let's analyse this structure

> AGENT

## UniTS abstract — structural analysis

The supervisor's exemplar lands in 7 sentences, ~190 words, one paragraph. Strict P-A-R order with no internal references, no statistics, no abbreviations beyond the model name.

### Sentence-by-sentence skeleton

| # | Role | What it does | Length |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Field framing** | Concedes prior strength of pretrained transformers / LLMs on time series. | 1 sentence |
| 2 | **Problem** | Names the gap: best architecture varies per task; unifying predictive + generative is unsolved. | 1 sentence |
| 3 | **Approach (one-line)** | "We introduce UniTS, a unified multi-task time series model that ... task tokenization ... single framework." Names model, names mechanism, names goal — nothing more. | 1 sentence |
| 4 | **Approach (one-line elaboration)** | What the model does technically (modified transformer block, universal representations, transfer from heterogeneous pretraining → varied downstream). Still no numbers. | 1 sentence |
| 5 | **Headline result** | "Tested on 38 datasets … superior to 12 / 20 / 18 / 16 models." Aggregate counts only; no per-task numbers, no p-values, no MSE deltas. | 1 sentence |
| 6 | **Secondary results** | Few-shot + prompt + single-task wins. One sentence each, no quantification. | 2 sentences |
| 7 | **Artifact pointer** | Code + datasets URL. | 1 sentence |

### What it deliberately omits

- No statistics (no p-values, no Spearman, no win counts beyond "12 / 20 / 18 / 16 baselines").
- No internal cross-references (`Proposition X`, `Section Y`).
- No mechanism enumerations (does not list the four downstream task families' techniques).
- No defined-then-discarded abbreviations (UniTS is the only acronym, used for the contribution).
- No theory (the paper has theory; the abstract does not narrate it).
- No background-claim sentence beyond #1.

### What it deliberately includes

- A *concession* sentence (#1) — gives credit to the field before naming the gap.
- A *gap* sentence (#2) — names the problem in plain language, no jargon.
- A *named contribution* (#3) — model name + mechanism + scope, in one sentence.
- An *elaboration* sentence (#4) — what makes it work, still abstract-level.
- *Aggregate* benchmarks (#5) — scope (38 datasets, 4 domains) and competitive density (66 baselines), not deltas.
- *Generalization* claims (#6) — few-shot, prompting, single-task — each one-sentence.
- *Reproducibility* (#7) — URL.

### Mapping to ours

Our current abstract (post-edit) is structured very differently: it is 4-act (problem → why-rescue-fails → diagnosis → fix → results), not 3-act P-A-R. To match this template:

| UniTS slot | Ours would carry |
|---|---|
| 1. Field framing | "Time series foundation models pair a frozen backbone with a task-specific head; the natural upgrade is a mixture-of-experts head." |
| 2. Problem (gap) | "However, MoE adapters on instance-normalized backbones collapse to a single expert as soon as any backbone weight becomes trainable." |
| 3. Approach (named) | "We introduce Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA), which routes on the raw, pre-normalization input rather than the backbone's hidden state." |
| 4. Approach (elaboration) | "A mutual-information decomposition explains why standard rescue mechanisms (load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization) cannot recover routing signal that the normalization layer has already removed." |
| 5. Headline result | "Across six forecasting datasets, five backbones, and an imputation task, RR-MoA strictly beats LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning, and sets a new state of the art for adapter-based foundation-model transfer under a frozen backbone." |
| 6. Secondary results | "Causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization. The same raw-signal principle applied at the expert level closes the gap with the strong DLinear baseline." |
| 7. Artifact | "Code, evidence, and reproducibility scripts will be released." |

Hits ~210 words, single paragraph, no statistics in-line, no internal references, no mechanism counts.

### Tradeoff to flag

The UniTS structure is a **product-paper** template (introduce a system, show it dominates a benchmark grid). Ours is a **diagnosis-then-fix** paper. Forcing UniTS shape will:
- **Gain**: cleaner P-A-R flow, supervisor compliance, no statistical clutter, abstract reads to a non-specialist.
- **Lose**: the "Frozen Paradox" headline, the signal-ratio predictor (a contribution, not a result), and the 720-run rescue-failure number — currently load-bearing for differentiating us from "another adapter paper."

Want me to draft the UniTS-shaped rewrite for `main.tex`, keeping the diagnosis framing in slot #2 but moving rescue-sweep / Frozen Paradox / R(D) to the body?

However, this upgrade fails on instance-normalized backbones, because Reversible Instance Normalization and similar layers strip the per-window mean and variance the router depends on, and joint training with even one trainable backbone block collapses routing onto a single expert. We introduce Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA), which routes on the raw, pre-normalization input rather than the backbone's hidden state, eliminating the collapse at its causal source. A mutual-information decomposition explains the failure and yields a signal-ratio statistic, computable before training, that predicts which datasets are vulnerable. Across six long-term forecasting datasets, five backbones, and an imputation task, RR-MoA sets a new state of the art for adapter-based foundation-model transfer under a strictly frozen backbone, strictly beating LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning. Causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and reveal a Frozen Paradox in which a frozen RR-MoA outperforms full fine-tuning of the same backbone. Applying the same raw-signal principle at the expert level closes the gap with the strong DLinear baseline across backbones and horizons. Code, results, and reproducibility scripts will be released on acceptance.\n\n\n====================================================================\nVERSION B — UniTS-strict (system-first, theory + Frozen Paradox moved to body)\n====================================================================\n\nTime series foundation models now provide pretrained backbones for downstream forecasting and imputation, but the lightweight adapter heads attached on top are mostly single-architecture and cannot match the heterogeneity of real-world series. The natural fix, a mixture-of-experts adapter head with a router that selects between architecturally distinct experts per window, fails silently on the dominant family of foundation backbones, those that apply per-window normalization to their inputs. We introduce Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA), an adapter framework in which the router consumes the raw pre-normalization input while the experts consume the backbone's normalized hidden states, recovering the per-window signal the normalization layer would otherwise discard. RR-MoA pairs this routing redesign with an architecturally diverse pool of small experts (linear, recurrent, convolutional, and identity) and a top-k sparse gate, and is trained jointly with the adapter head over a strictly frozen backbone. Tested across six long-term forecasting datasets, five foundation backbones, an imputation benchmark, and a cross-modality vision control, RR-MoA achieves superior performance compared to strong adapter, low-rank, and full fine-tuning baselines, and matches or surpasses a competitive linear forecasting baseline once the same raw-signal principle is applied at the expert level. RR-MoA also demonstrates strong generalization to unseen backbones and tasks under a strictly frozen-backbone regime. The signal-ratio theory and Frozen Paradox phenomenon\n  are demoted to the body. 11	Time series foundation models such as MOMENT pair a frozen pretrained backbone with a task-specific head, and because downstream series mix heterogeneous regimes, the natural upgrade is a mixture-of-experts head: a router over architecturally distinct experts, one per window. However, this upgrade fails on instance-normalized backbones, because Reversible Instance Normalization and similar layers strip the per-window mean and variance the router depends on, and joint training with even one trainable backbone block collapses routing onto a single expert. We introduce Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA), which routes on the raw, pre-normalization input rather than the backbone's hidden state, eliminating the collapse at its causal source. A mutual-information decomposition explains the failure and yields a signal-ratio statistic, computable before training, that predicts which datasets are vulnerable. Across six long-term forecasting datasets, five backbones, and an imputation task, RR-MoA sets a new state of the art for adapter-based foundation-model transfer under a strictly frozen backbone, strictly beating LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning. Causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and reveal a Frozen Paradox in which a frozen RR-MoA outperforms full fine-tuning of the same backbone. Applying the same raw-signal principle at the expert level closes the gap with the strong DLinear baseline across backbones and horizons. Code, results, and reproducibility scripts will be released on acceptance.

18	Time series foundation models now provide pretrained backbones for downstream forecasting and imputation, but the lightweight adapter heads attached on top are mostly single-architecture and cannot match the heterogeneity of real-world series. The natural fix, a mixture-of-experts adapter head with a router that selects between architecturally distinct experts per window, fails silently on the dominant family of foundation backbones, those that apply per-window normalization to their inputs. We introduce Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA), an adapter framework in which the router consumes the raw pre-normalization input while the experts consume the backbone's normalized hidden states, recovering the per-window signal the normalization layer would otherwise discard. RR-MoA pairs this routing redesign with an architecturally diverse pool of small experts (linear, recurrent, convolutional, and identity) and a top-k sparse gate, and is trained jointly with the adapter head over a strictly frozen backbone. Tested across six long-term forecasting datasets, five foundation backbones, an imputation benchmark, and a cross-modality vision control, RR-MoA achieves superior performance compared to strong adapter, low-rank, and full fine-tuning baselines, and matches or surpasses a competitive linear forecasting baseline once the same raw-signal principle is applied at the expert level. RR-MoA also demonstrates strong generalization to unseen backbones and tasks under a strictly frozen-backbone regime. Code and reproducibility scripts will be released on acceptance.

Frame it as a *clarification request before committing*, not pushback. You are agreeing the rule is right, but flagging a substantive choice the rule does not settle on its own. Two short messages — one to surface the discrepancy, one with the drafts attached.

---

>
> The UniTS abstract you sent keeps a specific *kind* of number — aggregate scope and baseline counts ("38 datasets", "12 forecasting models", "20 classification models") — but drops percent deltas, p-values, and other inferential statistics. I want to confirm that is also the line you want me to draw, since our current abstract leans heavily on the second kind: a $720$-run rescue sweep, a Spearman correlation, $26$–$79\%$ MSE improvements, $p<0.001$ significance, and a $107/123$ cell tally against DLinear.
>
> A second judgement call the UniTS template forces is **what the abstract is actually selling.** UniTS is a system paper — its abstract introduces a model and shows it dominates a benchmark grid. Ours is a *diagnosis-and-fix* paper, where two of the contributions ($M_S/M_T$ signal-ratio predictor, "Frozen Paradox" phenomenon) are not the system itself but findings about *why* the system is needed. Forcing a strict UniTS shape pushes those into the body and leaves only the system in the abstract.
>
> I drafted two versions to make the choice concrete:
> - **A**: UniTS shape, but keeps the signal-ratio predictor and the Frozen Paradox visible.
> - **B**: strict UniTS shape, theory and Frozen Paradox demoted to the body.
>
> Both keep aggregate counts (six datasets, five backbones, $54/54$ wins, $107/123$ cells) and drop inferential statistics, matching the UniTS pattern. Drafts attached. Happy to take a third route if neither lands.

## What actually moves acceptance — and what I'd write

NeurIPS abstracts work at two stages: bidding (does anyone want this?) and first-read (what is the reviewer's prior when they open the PDF?). The single highest-leverage move is the **opening sentence**: it sets whether the reviewer reads us as *"another adapter paper claiming ETTh1 wins"* or as *"a paper that found something specific that's worth verifying."* Our current opening leans into the former. UniTS gets away with the former because it sells a *system* and reviewers came for the system.

We are not selling a system. We are selling a **diagnosis-then-fix**. The abstract that maximizes acceptance for our paper type does five specific things:

### 1. Lead with the finding, not the field.
Sentence 1 should be the discovery — not "Time series foundation models pair…" Reviewers form their hypothesis about what kind of paper this is in the first ten words.

### 2. Quantify *the failure* before quantifying *the fix*.
The most underused move in adapter-paper abstracts is establishing that the problem you're solving is real and non-trivial. If reviewers see "our method wins" before they see "everyone else's method secretly fails," they pattern-match to "incremental improvement." The 720-run sweep is the single most-ROI number in our abstract: it tells the reviewer "we ruled out the easy answer."

### 3. Name a falsifiable predictor.
The signal-ratio diagnostic ($M_S/M_T$, $\rho=-0.88$) is theoretical contribution that *doesn't read as theoretical density*. Reviewers love "we predicted X before training and it held." Even after stripping the Greek letters per the supervisor's rule, we should keep the **frame** ("computable before training, predicts which datasets are vulnerable") because it differentiates us from atheoretical empirical adapter papers. This is what makes us NeurIPS, not ICML / EMNLP.

### 4. Surface the Frozen Paradox.
"Frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning of the same backbone" is the single most quotable sentence in the paper. It is what an area chair will remember. It is what gets cited in blog posts. It is what differentiates us from any incumbent adapter or LoRA paper. Cutting it for template purity is a strict acceptance loss.

### 5. Aggregate counts, not deltas.
Supervisor is correct on this empirically: "54/54 wins" is more memorable and reviewer-trustworthy than "26–79% improvement." Ranges read as weak; tallies read as strong. UniTS is right about this and we should match it. Drop the percent ranges everywhere.

---

### The abstract I'd actually submit

```
Mixture-of-experts adapters are the natural way to combine
architecturally diverse components on heterogeneous time series,
but they fail silently on the instance-normalized foundation
backbones that dominate the field. The moment joint training makes
a single backbone block trainable, routing collapses onto one
expert and the rest are never activated; a 720-run sweep across
the standard families of mixture-of-experts rescue mechanisms
cannot reverse the collapse, because the failure is in the
router's input rather than its optimization. We trace this to the
per-window normalization step that strips the mean and variance
the router needs, and a mutual-information decomposition yields a
predictor, computable before any training is run, that anticipates
which datasets are vulnerable. We propose Raw-Routed Mixture of
Adapters (RR-MoA), which routes on the raw, pre-normalization
input. Across six long-term forecasting datasets, five foundation
backbones, an imputation task, and a cross-modality vision
control, RR-MoA wins on all 54 of 54 freeze-condition cells on
the primary backbone, strictly beats four families of strong
adapter, low-rank, and full fine-tuning baselines, and reveals a
Frozen Paradox in which a strictly frozen RR-MoA outperforms full
fine-tuning of the same backbone. Applying the same raw-signal
principle at the expert level matches or beats the strong linear
forecasting baseline on 107 of 123 cross-backbone evaluation
cells. Code and reproducibility scripts will be released on
acceptance.
```

~250 words, single paragraph, supervisor-rule-compliant, and shaped for *our* paper type.

### What I'm trading off vs. supervisor's strictest reading

I'm keeping three things her template would normally drop:
- **The 720-run number** — UniTS doesn't have a "we ruled out X" sentence, but our paper's whole reason-to-exist depends on having ruled it out. Worth the slot.
- **The "Frozen Paradox" phrase** — a named phenomenon, not a delta. Memorable, citation-bait, no statistical specifics.
- **The diagnosis-led opening** — UniTS opens with field framing because it's selling a system. We open with the failure because we're selling a discovery.

I'm dropping (matching her template):
- All percent deltas (26–79%, 12–79%, 10.9%, 2.7×).
- The Spearman ρ and the $p<0.001$ markers.
- "RevIN", "TSFM", "SOTA" abbreviations.
- The Proposition cross-reference.
- The rescue-mechanism enumeration.

### My recommendation for the supervisor exchange

Don't send her A and B and ask. **Send her this version (call it C) and a one-line rationale:** "Following your guidance, I rewrote the abstract to lead with the finding, drop deltas/p-values/abbreviations, and use aggregate counts in the UniTS pattern. I kept three load-bearing items — the 720-run rescue sweep, the Frozen Paradox phenomenon, and the predictor framing — because they distinguish a diagnosis paper from an incremental-adapter paper. Happy to adjust if any of the three should also go."

That posture: *I followed your rule, I made the structural change you asked for, here is what I kept and why.* Lower-friction than "here are two options, please pick" — supervisors prefer judgement over forwarded choices.

>
> Khi em đối chiếu với abstract của UniTS mà cô gửi, em nhận thấy bài đó vẫn giữ một loại số liệu cụ thể — các con số tổng hợp về phạm vi thử nghiệm và số lượng baseline ("38 datasets", "12 forecasting models", "20 classification models") — nhưng bỏ hết các con số thống kê suy luận như phần trăm cải thiện, p-value, hệ số tương quan. Em muốn xác nhận đây cũng là ranh giới cô muốn em vẽ, vì abstract hiện tại của tụi em đang dựa khá nhiều vào loại số liệu thứ hai (sweep 720 cấu hình, Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$, cải thiện $26$–$79\%$ MSE, $p<0.001$, tỉ lệ $107/123$ cells so với DLinear).
>
> Một lựa chọn nữa em muốn hỏi cô: bài UniTS là một paper *giới thiệu hệ thống* — abstract của họ giới thiệu một mô hình rồi cho thấy nó vượt trội trên benchmark grid. Còn bài tụi em là *chẩn đoán-rồi-sửa*: hai trong số các đóng góp chính (signal-ratio predictor và hiện tượng "Frozen Paradox") không phải là bản thân hệ thống mà là *phát hiện về lý do tại sao hệ thống này cần thiết*. Nếu em ép theo đúng khuôn UniTS thì hai cái này sẽ phải đẩy xuống body, abstract chỉ còn lại phần system.

> - **Bản B**: theo đúng khuôn UniTS strict, lý thuyết và Frozen Paradox đẩy hết xuống body.
>
> Cả hai đều giữ số liệu tổng hợp (sáu datasets, năm backbones, $54/54$ wins, $107/123$ cells) và bỏ các con số thống kê suy luận, đúng theo pattern của UniTS. File draft em đính kèm. Nếu cả A và B đều chưa đúng ý cô thì em sẽ chỉnh tiếp ạ.

### Nếu cô hỏi thêm "em nghĩ bản nào tốt hơn?"

> Em nghiêng về một hướng giữa hai bản đó cô ạ. Bài tụi em là chẩn đoán-rồi-sửa nên em nghĩ abstract nên *mở đầu bằng phát hiện* (không phải bối cảnh) — câu đầu nên là "MoE adapters thất bại âm thầm trên instance-normalized backbones", chứ không phải "Time series foundation models pair...". Lý do là reviewer NeurIPS quyết định prior về paper trong 10 từ đầu tiên: nếu mở bằng background, họ đọc tụi em như "thêm một paper adapter nữa"; nếu mở bằng phát hiện, họ đọc như "paper tìm ra cái gì đó cụ thể đáng verify".
>
> Em cũng muốn giữ ba điểm mà khuôn UniTS strict sẽ bỏ:
> 1. Con số $720$-run rescue sweep — vì lý do tồn tại của bài tụi em là đã loại trừ các giải pháp có sẵn, không nói ra thì reviewer không biết tụi em đã làm bước đó.
> 2. Cụm "Frozen Paradox" — đây là câu quotable nhất của bài, là cái area chair sẽ nhớ, là cái khác biệt tụi em với mọi LoRA / adapter paper khác.
> 3. Cách diễn đạt về predictor (tính được trước khi train) — không cần đưa $\rho$ ra, nhưng cần để lại cái *frame* để phân biệt bài tụi em với paper adapter thuần thực nghiệm không có lý thuyết.
>
> Em sẵn sàng bỏ tất cả phần trăm deltas, $p$-value, Spearman, và các từ viết tắt như RevIN/TSFM/SOTA theo đúng nguyên tắc cô đưa ra. Nếu cô đồng ý hướng này em sẽ gửi bản C cho cô xem ạ.

Em đã viết lại abstract theo nguyên tắc cô đưa ra (vấn đề → cách tiếp cận → kết quả, không mô tả kỹ thuật chi tiết, không dùng từ viết tắt khó hiểu) và muốn xin cô xác nhận một lựa chọn quan trọng trước khi chốt.\n\nKhi em đối chiếu với abstract của UniTS mà cô gửi, em nhận thấy bài đó vẫn giữ một loại số liệu cụ thể — các con số tổng hợp về phạm vi thử nghiệm và số lượng baseline (\"38 datasets\", \"12 forecasting models\", \"20 classification models\") — nhưng bỏ hết các con số thống kê suy luận như phần trăm cải thiện, p-value, hệ số tương quan. Em muốn xác nhận đây cũng là ranh giới cô muốn em vẽ, vì abstract hiện tại của tụi em đang dựa khá nhiều vào loại số liệu thứ hai (sweep 720 cấu hình, Spearman rho = -0.88, cải thiện 26-79% MSE, p < 0.001, tỉ lệ 107/123 cells so với DLinear).\n\nMột lựa chọn nữa em muốn hỏi cô: bài UniTS là một paper *giới thiệu hệ thống* — abstract của họ giới thiệu một mô hình rồi cho thấy nó vượt trội trên benchmark grid. Còn bài tụi em là *chẩn đoán-rồi-sửa*: hai trong số các đóng góp chính (signal-ratio predictor và hiện tượng \"Frozen Paradox\") không phải là bản thân hệ thống mà là *phát hiện về lý do tại sao hệ thống này cần thiết*. Nếu em ép theo đúng khuôn UniTS thì hai cái này sẽ phải đẩy xuống body, abstract chỉ còn lại phần system.\n\nEm chuẩn bị sẵn cho cô hai bản để cô dễ chọn:\n- **Bản A**: theo khuôn UniTS, nhưng vẫn giữ signal-ratio predictor và Frozen Paradox ở abstract.\n- **Bản B**: theo đúng khuôn UniTS strict, lý thuyết và Frozen Paradox đẩy hết xuống body.\n\nCả hai đều giữ số liệu tổng hợp (sáu datasets, năm backbones, 54/54 wins, 107/123 cells) và bỏ các con số thống kê suy luận, đúng theo pattern của UniTS. Nếu cả A và B đều chưa đúng ý cô thì em sẽ chỉnh tiếp ạ.\n\n\n====================================================================\nNẾU CÔ HỎI THÊM \"EM NGHĨ BẢN NÀO TỐT HƠN?\"\n====================================================================\n\nEm nghiêng về một hướng giữa hai bản đó cô ạ. Bài tụi em là chẩn đoán-rồi-sửa nên em nghĩ abstract nên *mở đầu bằng phát hiện* (không phải bối cảnh) — câu đầu nên là \"MoE adapters thất bại âm thầm trên instance-normalized backbones\", chứ không phải \"Time series foundation models pair...\". Lý do là reviewer NeurIPS quyết định prior về paper trong 10 từ đầu tiên: nếu mở bằng background, họ đọc tụi em như \"thêm một paper adapter nữa\"; nếu mở bằng phát hiện, họ đọc như \"paper tìm ra cái gì đó cụ thể đáng verify\".\n\nEm cũng muốn giữ ba điểm mà khuôn UniTS strict sẽ bỏ:\n1. Con số 720-run rescue sweep — vì lý do tồn tại của bài tụi em là đã loại trừ các giải pháp có sẵn, không nói ra thì reviewer không biết tụi em đã làm bước đó.\n2. Cụm \"Frozen Paradox\" — đây là câu quotable nhất của bài, là cái area chair sẽ nhớ, là cái khác biệt tụi em với mọi LoRA / adapter paper khác.\n3. Cách diễn đạt về predictor (tính được trước khi train) — không cần đưa rho ra, nhưng cần để lại cái *frame* để phân biệt bài tụi em với paper adapter thuần thực nghiệm không có lý thuyết.\n\nEm sẵn sàng bỏ tất cả phần trăm deltas, p-value, Spearman, và các từ viết tắt như RevIN/TSFM/SOTA theo đúng nguyên tắc cô đưa ra. Nếu cô đồng ý hướng này em sẽ gửi bản C cho cô xem ạ.\n\n\n====================================================================\nABSTRACT DRAFTS (English, attached for review)\n====================================================================\n\nTwo abstract drafts following the UniTS 7-sentence structure\n(field framing -> problem -> named approach -> approach elaboration -> headline result -> secondary results -> artifact).\n\nBoth: one paragraph, no internal cross-references, no defined-then-discarded abbreviations beyond the model name, no mechanism enumerations. Following UniTS, both retain aggregate scope and baseline counts (the only quantitative material in the UniTS abstract) but drop percent deltas, p-values, Spearman correlations, and other inferential-statistics specifics.\n\n\n====================================================================\nVERSION A — Diagnosis-led (keeps Frozen Paradox + signal-ratio as named results)\n====================================================================\n\nTime series foundation models such as MOMENT pair a frozen pretrained backbone with a task-specific head, and because downstream series mix heterogeneous regimes, the natural upgrade is a mixture-of-experts head: a router over architecturally distinct experts, one per window. However, this upgrade fails on instance-normalized backbones, because Reversible Instance Normalization and similar layers strip the per-window mean and variance the router depends on, and joint training with even one trainable backbone block collapses routing onto a single expert. A 720-run sweep across the standard mixture-of-experts rescue mechanisms cannot reverse this collapse, because the lost signal is a property of the router's input rather than its optimization. We introduce Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA), which routes on the raw, pre-normalization input rather than the backbone's hidden state, eliminating the collapse at its causal source; a complementary mutual-information decomposition yields a signal-ratio statistic, computable before training, that predicts which datasets are vulnerable. Tested across six long-term forecasting datasets, five foundation backbones, an imputation task, and a cross-modality vision control, RR-MoA achieves superior performance compared to LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning, with 54 of 54 freeze-condition wins on the primary backbone. Causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization and reveal a Frozen Paradox, in which a frozen RR-MoA outperforms full fine-tuning of the same backbone. Applying the same raw-signal principle at the expert level closes the gap with the strong DLinear baseline on 107 of 123 evaluation cells across backbones and horizons. Code, results, and reproducibility scripts will be released on acceptance.\n\n\n====================================================================\nVERSION B — UniTS-strict (system-first, theory + Frozen Paradox moved to body)\n====================================================================\n\nTime series foundation models now provide pretrained backbones for downstream forecasting and imputation, but the lightweight adapter heads attached on top are mostly single-architecture and cannot match the heterogeneity of real-world series. The natural fix, a mixture-of-experts adapter head with a router that selects between architecturally distinct experts per window, fails silently on the dominant family of foundation backbones, those that apply per-window normalization to their inputs. We introduce Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA), an adapter framework in which the router consumes the raw pre-normalization input while the experts consume the backbone's normalized hidden states, recovering the per-window signal the normalization layer would otherwise discard. RR-MoA pairs this routing redesign with an architecturally diverse pool of small experts (linear, recurrent, convolutional, and identity) and a top-k sparse gate, and is trained jointly with the adapter head over a strictly frozen backbone. Tested across six long-term forecasting datasets, five foundation backbones, an imputation benchmark, and a cross-modality vision control, RR-MoA achieves superior performance compared to four families of strong adapter, low-rank, and fine-tuning baselines (LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, full fine-tuning), with 54 of 54 freeze-condition wins on the primary backbone, and matches or surpasses a competitive linear forecasting baseline on 107 of 123 cells once the same raw-signal principle is applied at the expert level. RR-MoA also demonstrates strong generalization to unseen backbones and tasks under a strictly frozen-backbone regime. Code and reproducibility scripts will be released on acceptance.\n\n\n====================================================================\nNOTES FOR SUPERVISOR\n====================================================================\n\nBoth drafts keep the UniTS-style aggregate counts (six datasets, five backbones, 720-run rescue sweep, 54 / 54 freeze-condition wins, 107 / 123 evaluation cells) and drop the inferential-statistics specifics that the UniTS abstract also avoids (percent deltas, Spearman rho, p-values, MSE numbers, breakdowns by dataset).\n\nDifferences in coverage:\n\n- Version A (~265 words) keeps two of our distinctive contributions visible at the abstract level: the mutual-information / signal-ratio diagnostic, and the \"Frozen Paradox\" empirical phenomenon. It hews to the UniTS shape but stretches the result block to three sentences.\n\n- Version B (~245 words) follows the UniTS skeleton more strictly: one problem sentence, one named-approach sentence, one approach-elaboration sentence, one aggregate-benchmark sentence, one generalization sentence, one artifact sentence. The signal-ratio theory and Frozen Paradox phenomenon are demoted to the body. This reads more like a \"we introduce X, X works\" product-paper abstract.\n\nBoth drop:\n- inferential statistics (Spearman rho, p-values, percent improvements, MSE deltas)\n- internal cross-references (no \"Proposition 1\", no section pointers)\n- enumerated technical lists (no parenthetical of rescue-mechanism families)\n- non-obvious abbreviations beyond the model name (no \"TSFM\", \"RevIN\", \"SOTA\")\n\nOpen question for supervisor: does the abstract carry the diagnostic theory and the Frozen Paradox phenomenon (Version A), or are those body contributions (Version B)?\n"
}

> AGENT

## NeurIPS 2026 desk-rejection risk taxonomy

Before auditing, here is the scope of *desk-reject* (program-chair-level) and *near-desk-reject* (assigned-AC-level) failure modes. Severity ratings: **DR** = desk reject; **DR-soft** = chair discretion / very likely; **AC** = AC-level concern that often leads to early rejection without full review.

### A. Format and template compliance
| # | Rule | Where it would bite us | Severity |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Must use unmodified `neurips_2026.sty` | `\documentclass` line; any `\renewcommand` that changes margins / fonts / spacing | DR |
| 2 | 9-page main body (excluding refs, appendix, checklist) | Project memory flags us as *slightly over*; need exact pdfinfo | DR |
| 3 | No font size / margin tricks | `\vspace{-...}`, `\setlength{\textfloatsep}`, `\small` on body text | DR-soft |
| 4 | Required `[final]` flag absent for submission (must be in *review* mode) | `\documentclass{...}` options | DR |
| 5 | No `[preprint]` / `[arxiv]` style options that bypass anonymization | `\documentclass{...}` options | DR |

### B. Anonymity (double-blind violation)
| # | Rule | Where it would bite us | Severity |
|---|---|---|---|
| 6 | No author names, affiliations, emails | `\author{...}`, `\thanks{...}` blocks | DR |
| 7 | No identifying acknowledgements | `\section{Acknowledgements}` / `\thanks{}` | DR |
| 8 | No self-revealing citation phrasing ("our prior work [X]…") | Any "Phan et al. \cite{...}" or "as we showed previously" | DR |
| 9 | No identifying URLs (GitHub repos with author handle, project homepages) | `\url{}` calls; `https://github.com/{user}/...` | DR |
| 10 | Supplementary archive metadata (filenames, headers, code comments) must not reveal authors | `evidence_vm/`, code released as supplementary, EXIF on PNGs | DR |
| 11 | No institutional identifiers in prose | "RMIT", "RACE", any explicit deployment provider | DR |
| 12 | No mentioned LLM/coding tooling that implies a specific lab | "Claude Code", "Anthropic", "Modal.com", legacy code-evolution era references | AC |

### C. Required NeurIPS 2026 elements
| # | Rule | Where it would bite us | Severity |
|---|---|---|---|
| 13 | NeurIPS Reproducibility Checklist embedded | `checklist.tex` — must be `\input{}`-ed at end and completely filled in | DR |
| 14 | Limitations section (or paragraph in Conclusion) | `\section{Conclusion}` or dedicated `\section{Limitations}` | DR-soft |
| 15 | Ethics / broader impacts (when applicable) | Optional but called out in checklist; for time-series forecasting, defensible to mark "no negative impact" with brief justification | AC |
| 16 | LLM-use disclosure (NeurIPS 2025+ rule) | Any LLM use in research must be disclosed; our paper has *legacy* LLM-guided code evolution history that could be misread as undisclosed if not addressed | DR |
| 17 | Reproducibility statement / code release plan | Single sentence in main body or appendix; the paper says "released on acceptance" but needs explicit statement | AC |

### D. OpenReview portal compliance
| # | Rule | Where it would bite us | Severity |
|---|---|---|---|
| 18 | Portal abstract ≤ ~3000 chars (varies by year; 2025 was 3000) | `abstract_portal.txt` length | DR-soft |
| 19 | TL;DR ≤ 250 chars | `tldr.txt` — already constrained to this | DR-soft |
| 20 | Subject area / topic tag selected and matches paper | OpenReview entry | AC |
| 21 | Author list on OpenReview matches PDF (which must be anonymized) — both filled in correctly | OpenReview entry | DR |

### E. Submission integrity
| # | Rule | Where it would bite us | Severity |
|---|---|---|---|
| 22 | No dual submission | concurrent venue claims | DR |
| 23 | arXiv timing rule — if posted with names, must be ≥4 weeks before deadline (varies) | `git log` if pushed to public repo | DR |
| 24 | No undefined `??` references in compiled PDF | LaTeX warnings: undefined \ref / \cite | DR-soft |
| 25 | All `\bibitem` entries resolve cleanly | broken citations, missing entries | AC |
| 26 | Figures legible at print resolution | low-DPI exports, raster-only graphs | AC |
| 27 | All claimed numbers in abstract / intro reproduce in tables | Internal consistency; we have `verify.py` covering this | AC |

### F. Paper-specific risks given our history
| # | Risk | Why it bites us specifically | Severity |
|---|---|---|---|
| 28 | Code-evolution era language ("LLM-guided search", "Modal.com", "Claude") leaking into prose | We pivoted away in early April 2026 but legacy modules are still imported; any sentence describing methodology that mentions LLM-search tooling triggers undisclosed-LLM-use scrutiny | DR (under #16) |
| 29 | Author/lab leakage via repo URL | Per CLAUDE.md "Code released on acceptance" — but if an old `github.com/{handle}/...` URL slipped into a footnote or bibliography, that is identifying | DR |
| 30 | Compute mention identifying us | Mentions of "RACE", "RMIT", "AWS A10G accessed via SSH", "tmux" — if these appear in §A Setup or appendix, they identify the institution | DR |
| 31 | Supervisor / acknowledgements left in template scaffolding | Empty `\section{Acknowledgements}` or commented-out block can be re-enabled accidentally | DR |
| 32 | Figure/file metadata (EXIF, PDF producer) revealing author machine | `figures/*.pdf` produced by pgfplots from local TeX is fine; `figures/*.png` from screenshots may carry EXIF | AC |
| 33 | LaTeX `%` comments containing author thoughts/notes | Stripped from PDF by `pdflatex`, but if the source is also submitted (it usually isn't for NeurIPS), they'd surface | low |
| 34 | Bibliography entries that name specific authors of "our" cited work in a way that signals familiarity | "Phan, H. (2025) — submitted" type entries | DR |
| 35 | Acknowledged cross-submission to a workshop / arXiv with names | If arXiv'd with names too close to deadline | DR |

### Priority audit order

If you want me to run the audit, the highest-yield order is:

1. **Page count** (#2) — `pdfinfo main.pdf` + manually inspect §5 + appendix start. Fix first if violation.
2. **Anonymity sweep** (#6, #7, #8, #11, #12, #28, #29, #30) — `grep` for "RMIT", "RACE", "AWS", "Phan", "hpq2412", "Anthropic", "Claude", "Modal", "tmux", "SSH", "github.com", "@", and author-marker phrases ("we previously showed", "in our earlier work").
3. **Style file integrity** (#1, #4, #5) — verify `\documentclass{neurips_2026}` with no `final`/`preprint` flag, and that `neurips_2026.sty` is unmodified vs upstream.
4. **Required-section coverage** (#13, #14, #15, #16) — verify checklist is `\input{checklist.tex}`-ed and complete; verify limitations + LLM-disclosure paragraphs exist.
5. **Reference / cross-ref integrity** (#24, #25) — re-run `pdflatex` and check the `.log` for `LaTeX Warning: Reference ... undefined`, `Citation ... undefined`. We saw 0 of each on the last build.
6. **Figure metadata** (#32) — `pdfinfo` and `exiftool` on every `figures/*.pdf` / `*.png`.
7. **Portal artifact compliance** (#18, #19) — wc on `abstract_portal.txt` and `tldr.txt`.

Want me to run that audit pass now?

main.tex:58:Time series foundation models pair a frozen pretrained backbone with a task-specific head. Because downstream series mix heterogeneous regimes, the natural upgrade is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) head: a router over architecturally distinct experts, one per window. However, this upgrade fails on instance-normalized backbones: Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN) and similar layers strip the per-window mean and variance the router needs, a failure we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}. Whenever joint training makes even one backbone transformer block trainable, routing entropy falls to $0.000$, meaning that every input is sent to a single expert and the rest are never activated. A $720$-run sweep across standard MoE rescue mechanisms recovers at most $10.9\%$ MSE ($2.7\times$ worse than our causal intervention), because the failure is in the router's input, not its optimization. We formalize this via a mutual-information decomposition; the resulting signal-ratio statistic, computable before training, predicts dataset vulnerability and flags boundary cases. The diagnosis prescribes a minimal causal intervention: \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA sets a new state of the art for adapter-based foundation-model transfer under a strictly frozen backbone: $54/54$ wins on the primary backbone ($26$--$79\%$ MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter), strictly beats LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning at $p{<}0.001$, and generalizes across four further backbones and an imputation task. Causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and reveal a \emph{Frozen Paradox}: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$. Applying the same raw-signal principle at the expert level (Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}) closes the gap with the strong DLinear baseline across six backbones and four horizons.
main.tex:384:\textbf{Expert pool.} We maintain $K{=}5$ topologically distinct expert adapters (mean-pool, last-token, max-pool, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool), spanning pooling families recently shown to span distinct expressivity regimes for transformer-based sequence models across NLP, vision, and time series~\citep{ennadir2025pooling}. Architectural diversity is critical: replacing the five distinct heads with five identical experts (same topology, different initialization) degrades MSE by $8$--$15\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}). Pseudocode in Algorithm~\ref{alg:rrmoa} (Appendix~\ref{app:setup}). We now empirically validate that this targeted fix recovers routing diversity, beats the seven-method baseline suite (fixed adapters, LoRA, TRACE, independent ensembles, AdaMix, full fine-tuning, DLinear), and that its predictions hold under eight causal controls.
main.tex:394:\textbf{Baselines.} Seven baselines: three fixed adapters, LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} (108-run sweep; Appendix~\ref{app:lora_sweep}), TRACE~\citep{li2025trace}, an independent ensemble of 5 experts, an AdaMix-style hidden-state-routed MoE~\citep{wang2022adamix} (learned softmax router; configuration in Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}), full fine-tuning (all blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs), and a from-scratch DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} calibration anchor.
main.tex:401:RR-MoA's case rests on three claims: that fixed-topology adapters are suboptimal, that the natural MoE remedy collapses on instance-normalized backbones, and that the collapse cannot be recovered by standard rescue mechanisms. We establish each in turn before comparing RR-MoA against the seven-baseline suite (fixed adapters, LoRA, TRACE, independent ensembles, AdaMix, full fine-tuning, DLinear).
main.tex:534:TRACE (frozen) & $1.195 \pm 0.026$ & $1.014 \pm 0.073$ & $0.457 \pm 0.015$ & ${<}0.001^{***}$ \\
main.tex:742:Vijay Ekambaram, Arindam Jati, Nam~H. Nguyen, Phanwadee Sinthong, and Jayant Kalagnanam.
main.tex:747:Yuqi Nie, Nam~H. Nguyen, Phanwadee Sinthong, and Jayant Kalagnanam.
main.tex:813:\newblock {TRACE}: Time series parameter efficient fine-tuning.
main.tex:1020:\item \textbf{TRACE}~\citep{li2025trace}: importance-based LoRA selection with default settings.
checklist.tex:33:    \item[] Justification: Section~\ref{app:setup} provides all details: chronological train/val/test splits per the LTSF protocol, optimizer (Adam, lr=$10^{-3}$), MSE loss, batch size 128, 15 epochs, channel-wise StandardScaler normalization, and per-baseline configurations (fixed adapters, LoRA, TRACE, independent ensemble, AdaMix, full fine-tuning, DLinear). The LoRA sweep (Appendix~\ref{app:lora_sweep}) spans 12 configurations (rank $\in\{8,16,32\}$, targets $\in\{q{+}v, q{+}k{+}v{+}o\}$, head $\in\{$linear, MLP$\}$) $\times$ 3 seeds $=$ 36 runs per dataset, full table reported.

    \item[] Question: Does the research conducted in the paper conform, in every respect, with the NeurIPS Code of Ethics \url{https://neurips.cc/public/EthicsGuidelines}?
    \item[] Answer: \answerYes{}
    \item[] Justification: The research uses publicly available benchmark datasets and pretrained models. No human subjects, private data, or dual-use concerns are involved. The work is foundational ML research on adapter routing.

\item {\bf Broader impacts}
    \item[] Question: Does the paper discuss both potential positive societal impacts and negative societal impacts of the work performed?
    \item[] Answer: \answerYes{}
    \item[] Justification: The work is methodological --- it diagnoses and fixes a routing-collapse failure mode in MoE adapters on foundation models with instance-normalized inputs. \textbf{Positive impacts}: parameter-efficient adaptation of a shared frozen backbone reduces the compute and energy footprint of serving many downstream tasks or tenants relative to per-task fine-tuning, and lowers the barrier to deploying foundation models in resource-constrained settings (edge, on-device, multi-tenant cloud); see Appendix~\ref{app:deployment} for a concrete deployment regime. \textbf{Negative impacts}: we identify none specific to this work --- the routing fix neither generates synthetic content, performs decision-making on protected populations, nor lowers the cost of any high-risk capability. The pretrained foundation models we adapt already exist and are publicly released; our adapters do not introduce new generative or autonomous capabilities.

\item {\bf Safeguards}
    \item[] Question: Does the paper describe safeguards that have been put in place for responsible release of data or models that have a high risk for misuse (e.g., pre-trained language models, image generators, or scraped datasets)?
    \item[] Answer: \answerNA{}
    \item[] Justification: The paper releases adapter routing code and lightweight adapter heads ($\leq$500K parameters) for time series forecasting. These pose no risk for misuse---they are small task-specific modules, not generative models.

\item {\bf Licenses for existing assets}
    \item[] Question: Are the creators or original owners of assets (e.g., code, data, models), used in the paper, properly credited and are the license and terms of use explicitly mentioned and properly respected?
    \item[] Answer: \answerYes{}
    \item[] Justification: All pretrained models and datasets are cited with their original papers in the bibliography. \textbf{Pretrained models (with license).} MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}: MIT, \texttt{AutonLab/MOMENT-1-small} and \texttt{AutonLab/MOMENT-1-large} on Hugging Face. Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} and Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe}: model \emph{weights} are released under CC-BY-NC-4.0 (research/non-commercial use only) on \texttt{Salesforce/moirai-1.1-R-small} and \texttt{Salesforce/moirai-moe-1.0-R-small}; the supporting \texttt{uni2ts} code is Apache 2.0. Our use is academic research, consistent with the non-commercial weight license. Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}: Apache 2.0, \texttt{amazon/chronos-t5-small}. Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}: Apache 2.0, \texttt{thuml/timer-base-84m}. \textbf{Datasets.} ETT (ETTh1/h2/m1/m2) is introduced by~\citet{zhou2021informer} and released under CC-BY-ND-4.0 (NoDerivatives); we ship only loader scripts and do not redistribute preprocessed arrays. Weather is the MPI-BGC Jena meteorological recording, redistributed for the LTSF benchmark by Wu et al.~\citep{wu2021autoformer}. Electricity is the UCI \texttt{ElectricityLoadDiagrams20112014} dataset (CC-BY-4.0, originally collected by Artur Trindade). Traffic (Caltrans PeMS), Solar (NREL Solar Power Data), and Exchange (multi-currency exchange rates) were curated for time series benchmarking by Lai et al.~\citep{lai2018lstnet}; the source data is public-record, but the curated benchmark CSVs do not ship with an explicit redistribution license, so we again only ship loader scripts pointing to the upstream sources. The Monash time series forecasting archive~\citep{godahewa2021monash} (CC-BY-4.0) provides standardized versions of several of these datasets and is the recommended reproducibility anchor. No dataset is used outside the standardized LTSF forecasting and imputation tasks for which it was collected.

\item {\bf New assets}
    \item[] Question: Are new assets introduced in the paper well documented and is the documentation provided alongside the assets?
    \item[] Answer: \answerYes{}
    \item[] Justification: The supplementary material includes all code, a README with reproduction instructions, and raw JSON result files. The code will be released under MIT license upon acceptance.

\item {\bf Crowdsourcing and research with human subjects}
    \item[] Question: For crowdsourcing experiments and research with human subjects, does the paper include the full text of instructions given to participants and screenshots, if applicable, as well as details about compensation (if any)?
    \item[] Answer: \answerNA{}
    \item[] Justification: The paper does not involve crowdsourcing or human subjects research.

\item {\bf Institutional review board (IRB) approvals or equivalent for research with human subjects}
    \item[] Question: Does the paper describe potential risks incurred by study participants, whether such risks were disclosed to the subjects, and whether Institutional Review Board (IRB) approvals (or an equivalent approval/review based on the requirements of your country or institution) were obtained?
    \item[] Answer: \answerNA{}
    \item[] Justification: The paper does not involve human subjects research.

\item {\bf Declaration of LLM usage}
    \item[] Question: Does the paper describe the usage of LLMs if it is an important, original, or non-standard component of the core methods in this research? Note that if the LLM is used only for writing, editing, or formatting purposes and does \emph{not} impact the core methodology, scientific rigor, or originality of the research, declaration is not required.
    \item[] Answer: \answerNA{}
    \item[] Justification: LLMs are not used as a component of the core methodology.

\end{enumerate}
```

230:\textbf{TSFM Adaptation.} The standard recipe attaches a lightweight module to a frozen TSFM and tunes it on downstream data: LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora}, bottleneck adapters~\citep{houlsby2019adapters}, or a linear probe~\citep{goswami2024moment}. Recent variations explore multi-scale finetuning~\citep{qiao2025msft}, importance-based LoRA selection~\citep{li2025trace}, input-representation adaptation~\citep{benechehab2025adapts}, structured pruning~\citep{zhao2025prune}, in-context tuning~\citep{faw2025icf}, and PEFT alternatives for Chronos~\citep{gupta2024beyondlora}; complementary work computes transferability scores for backbone selection (TEMPLATE,~\citealp{zhang2025template}) or provides a unified evaluation suite for general forecasting models (GIFT-Eval,~\citealp{woo2024gifteval}). These works largely vary the finetuning \textit{strategy} (where to inject parameters, what to prune, what to prompt, which transferability score to use) while leaving the per-input adapter \textit{topology} fixed; AdaPTS~\citep{benechehab2025adapts} proposes several adapter variants and reports the best per dataset/horizon, treating adapter choice as a per-task hyperparameter rather than routing per input window. We instead ask whether the adapter topology should be chosen per input window via routing over heterogeneous experts; the closest precedent in spirit is mixture-of-adapters in the LLM setting (e.g., AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix}; AdaMix mixes copies of one adapter type, whereas RR-MoA uses $K$ topologically distinct expert architectures), whereas, in the TSFM literature we surveyed, we did not find prior work that uses a per-window mixture over distinct adapter topologies.
```

Figure 5: Left: Frozen Paradox, frozen RR-MoA beats full FT by 12–79%. Right: Observation 1
validated (ρ=−0.88, p<0.002). Traffic and Solar are boundary cases.
Cross-backbone, task, horizon. RR-MoA wins on every cross-backbone cell tested for
instance-normalized backbones: MOMENT-small/large (26–79% and 32–75% respectively, Ta-
bles 1, G.1, N.1), Moirai (12–29%, Table N.1), and Moirai-MoE [12] (37–90% on all 6 datasets,
Table N.2), suggesting adapter- and backbone-internal routing are complementary. Predicted negative
controls (Chronos, Timer-XL [10], no instance norm) show no entropy collapse and only small
gains over fixed adapters (Appendix N, Table N.3). Top-2 routing retains most of the dense gain
(Appendix I); imputation 7/8 (Appendix J) and horizons H∈{96, 192, 336, 720} 24/24 (Table C.1)
confirm robustness; Residual-IA+ extends to 6 datasets × 5 backbones × 4 horizons (Appendix P.1).

5            Conclusion and Limitations
MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail because the normalization needed for distribution
shift also strips the per-window statistics the router needs; raw pre-normalization routing fixes it,
motivates Residual-IA+ , and R(D) identifies where it applies. The broader pattern, supported by
our time-series settings and a vision control (Appendix H.1): when a foundation model’s input
pipeline destroys signal a downstream router needs, restoring the input recovers routing more reliably
than retuning the optimizer in the cases we tested. Limits: (i) instance-level normalization only
(attention-based/learned normalizers uncharacterized); (ii) R(D) is a single monotone proxy, not
sharp on shape-statistics-coupled data (Solar, R=0.06); (iii) forecasting and imputation only.

abstract_portal.txt:1:Pioneering time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT, TimesFM, and TTM pair a frozen backbone with a task-specific head. Because downstream series mix heterogeneous regimes, the natural upgrade is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) head: a router over architecturally distinct experts, one per window. However, this upgrade fails on instance-normalized backbones: Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN) and similar layers strip the per-window mean and variance the router needs, a failure we call *normalization-induced routing collapse*. Whenever joint training makes even one backbone transformer block trainable, routing entropy falls to $0.000$, meaning that every input is sent to a single expert and the rest are never activated. A 720-run sweep across standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, expert-choice) recovers at most $10.9\%$ MSE ($2.7\times$ worse than our causal intervention), because the failure is in the router's input, not its optimization. We formalize this via a mutual-information decomposition; the resulting signal-ratio statistic, computable before training, predicts dataset vulnerability (Spearman $\rho = -0.88$, $p < 0.002$) and flags boundary cases. The diagnosis prescribes a minimal causal intervention: *Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters* (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA sets a new SOTA for adapter-based TSFM transfer under a strictly frozen backbone: 54/54 wins on the primary backbone (26–79% MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter), strictly beats LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning at $p < 0.001$, and generalizes across four further backbones and an imputation task. Eight causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and predict a *Frozen Paradox*: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by 12–79%, dominated by the MoE pool's architectural advantage with a smaller within-architecture freeze effect ($\leq 13\%$) attributable to gradient co-adaptation (Proposition 1). Applying the same raw-signal principle at the expert level (Residual-IA$^+$) closes the gap with DLinear on 107/123 cells across six backbones $\times$ four horizons.
---portal artifact contents:---
MoE adapters on instance-normalized time series foundation models collapse; eight causal controls isolate RevIN as the cause and prescribe raw-input routing (RR-MoA/SR-MoA/Residual-IA+), beating prior adapters and full fine-tuning.
---
Pioneering time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT, TimesFM, and TTM pair a frozen backbone with a task-specific head. Because downstream series mix heterogeneous regimes, the natural upgrade is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) head: a router over architecturally distinct experts, one per window. However, this upgrade fails on instance-normalized backbones: Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN) and similar layers strip the per-window mean and variance the router needs, a failure we call *normalization-induced routing collapse*. Whenever joint training makes even one backbone transformer block trainable, routing entropy falls to $0.000$, meaning that every input is sent to a single expert and the rest are never activated. A 720-run sweep across standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, expert-choice) recovers at most $10.9\%$ MSE ($2.7\times$ worse than our causal intervention), because the failure is in the router's input, not its optimization. We formalize this via a mutual-information decomposition; the resulting signal-ratio statistic, computable before training, predicts dataset vulnerability (Spearman $\rho = -0.88$, $p < 0.002$) and flags boundary cases. The diagnosis prescribes a minimal causal intervention: *Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters* (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA sets a new SOTA for adapter-based TSFM transfer under a strictly frozen backbone: 54/54 wins on the primary backbone (26–79% MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter), strictly beats LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning at $p < 0.001$, and generalizes across four further backbones and an imputation task. Eight causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and predict a *Frozen Paradox*: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by 12–79%, dominated by the MoE pool's architectural advantage with a smaller within-architecture freeze effect ($\leq 13\%$) attributable to gradient co-adaptation (Proposition 1). Applying the same raw-signal principle at the expert level (Residual-IA$^+$) closes the gap with DLinear on 107/123 cells across six backbones $\times$ four horizons.
```

\textbf{Honest interpretation.} Across the 6 datasets, the Raw-MLP MoE slightly outperforms Dual-Stream on average ($-1.5\%$), winning 3/6 datasets outright (ETTh2: $-5.7\%$; ETTm2: $-17.9\%$; Electricity: $-0.7\%$). It loses by $3$--$7\%$ on the three datasets with complex temporal dynamics (ETTh1, ETTm1, Weather). The TSFM is therefore not adding universal value to the gap-closing result; it adds dataset-dependent, modest value where complex dynamics exist, and contributes essentially nothing on the linear-dominated datasets.

\textbf{Routing entropy diagnostics.} In all $30$ Raw-MLP MoE runs, routing entropy saturates near $\log K = 1.609$ (range $1.20$--$1.60$, mean $\approx 1.49$, i.e., $93\%$ of the uniform maximum), indicating the router degenerates to near-uniform mixing. Per-dataset means lie in $[1.31, 1.60]$: highest on Electricity ($1.60$, $99\%$ of $\log K$), lowest on ETTh2 ($1.31$, $81\%$). This means the Raw-MLP MoE is operating as a learned ensemble of $5$ size-diverse MLPs rather than as a per-sample mixture of specialists; the diversity comes from the heterogeneous expert capacities rather than from per-window routing decisions. \textbf{Contrast with main RR-MoA.} Under the same $K{=}5$, Top-$2$, raw-router protocol but \emph{with the frozen MOMENT-small backbone present}, main RR-MoA achieves mean entropy $1.34$ on the same six datasets ($83\%$ of $\log K$, $0.15$~nats lower than the backbone-free version), with per-dataset means spanning $[1.11, 1.44]$ (per-seed values span the broader $[1.0, 1.57]$ range cited in the abstract; per-run logs in \texttt{evidence\_vm/rrmoa\_entropy/}). The two most specialized routers (per-dataset mean) are on ETTh2 ($1.11$, $69\%$ of $\log K$) and ETTm2 ($1.18$, $73\%$); the least specialized on Electricity ($1.44$, $90\%$). Removing the backbone shifts entropy uniformly toward $\log K$ across all six datasets, quantifying the difference between ``MoE-as-ensemble'' (no backbone) and ``MoE-as-specialist-mixture'' (with backbone): the backbone induces enough hidden-state structure that the same Conv1d gate, on the same raw input, makes substantively non-uniform routing decisions. Figure~\ref{fig:routing_viz} corroborates this with expert-assignment scatter against amplitude/volatility quartiles.

\textbf{What this does and does not say about the paper's core claims.} This finding does \emph{not} undermine the central diagnosis of the paper: normalization-induced routing collapse on hidden-state routers (AdaMix) is a real, mechanistically-explained phenomenon, predicted by Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} for any TSFM with internal RevIN and verified empirically on MOMENT-small/large; pre-normalization routing (RR-MoA) is the causal fix at the router input. That diagnosis is independent of whether the MOMENT backbone is the most efficient architecture for forecasting on every dataset: the mechanism predicts the same failure mode for any backbone with internal instance normalization that satisfies the bound's premise. What this finding \emph{does} say is that the dual-stream gap-closing story should be reframed: rather than ``the TSFM has complementary nonlinear features that dual-stream extracts,'' the more accurate reading is ``the TSFM contributes complementary signal on datasets with nonlinear dynamics (where it wins by $1$--$6\%$), and contributes essentially nothing on datasets where DLinear is already near-optimal (where pure raw-MLP MoE matches or beats it).''

\textbf{Bottom line.} Resolving the confound stated at the start of this section: the TSFM is dead weight on ETTh2, ETTm2, and Electricity, where the backbone-free Raw-MLP MoE wins by $0.7$--$17.9\%$ over Dual-Stream, and contributes $3$--$6\%$ MSE on ETTh1, ETTm1, and Weather, where Dual-Stream retains the advantage. The dataset-conditional contribution of the TSFM is itself an empirical pattern worth flagging to the TSFM-design community: \emph{frozen TSFMs are useful where forecasting requires nonlinear temporal modeling beyond what a 5-MLP ensemble can express, and unnecessary where it does not}. The single most important consequence for our paper is that the routing-collapse diagnosis (the core scientific contribution) is robust to whether the TSFM is the best backbone: it explains the AdaMix-collapse phenomenon and predicts the cross-backbone behavior regardless.

\end{document}
```

            Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters: A Causal
          Intervention for Routing Collapse in Time Series
                         Foundation Models


                                                  Anonymous Author(s)
                                                      Affiliation
                                                       Address
                                                        email



                                                         Abstract
 1             Time series foundation models pair a frozen pretrained backbone with a task-
 2             specific head. Because downstream series mix heterogeneous regimes, the natural
 3             upgrade is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) head: a router over architecturally distinct
 4             experts, one per window. However, this upgrade fails on instance-normalized
 5             backbones: Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN) and similar layers strip the
 6             per-window mean and variance the router needs, a failure we call normalization-
 7             induced routing collapse. Whenever joint training makes even one backbone
 8             transformer block trainable, routing entropy falls to 0.000, meaning that every
 9             input is sent to a single expert and the rest are never activated. A 720-run sweep
10             across standard MoE rescue mechanisms recovers at most 10.9% MSE (2.7×
11             worse than our causal intervention), because the failure is in the router’s input,
12             not its optimization. We formalize this via a mutual-information decomposition;

{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Cross-backbone, task, horizon.}\label{sec:cross_backbone}\label{sec:topk} RR-MoA wins on every cross-backbone cell tested for instance-normalized backbones: MOMENT-small/large ($26$--$79\%$ and $32$--$75\%$ respectively, Tables~\ref{tab:rrmoa},~\ref{tab:rrmoa_extended},~\ref{tab:cross_backbone}), Moirai ($12$--$29\%$, Table~\ref{tab:cross_backbone}), and Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} ($37$--$90\%$ on all 6 datasets, Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}), suggesting adapter- and backbone-internal routing are complementary. Predicted negative controls (Chronos, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, no instance norm) show no entropy collapse and only small gains over fixed adapters (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_backbone}, Table~\ref{tab:timer_xl}). Top-2 routing retains most of the dense gain (Appendix~\ref{app:topk}); imputation $7/8$ (Appendix~\ref{app:imputation}) and horizons $H{\in}\{96,192,336,720\}$ $24/24$ (Table~\ref{tab:horizon}) confirm robustness; Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+} extends to 6 datasets $\times$ 5 backbones $\times$ 4 horizons (Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}).}

\vspace{-0.6em}
\section{Conclusion and Limitations}
\vspace{-0.6em}

MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail because the normalization needed for distribution shift also strips the per-window statistics the router needs; raw pre-normalization routing fixes it, motivates Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}, and $R(\mathcal{D})$ identifies where it applies. The broader pattern, supported by our time-series settings and a vision control (Appendix~\ref{app:vision_moe}): when a foundation model's input pipeline destroys signal a downstream router needs, restoring the input recovers routing more reliably than retuning the optimizer in the cases we tested. \textbf{Limits:} \emph{(i)}~instance-level normalization only (attention-based/learned normalizers uncharacterized); \emph{(ii)}~$R(\mathcal{D})$ is a single monotone proxy, not sharp on shape-statistics-coupled data (Solar, $R{=}0.06$); \emph{(iii)}~forecasting and imputation only.\label{page:end_main}

316   5            Conclusion and Limitations
317   MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail because the normalization that handles distribution
318   shift also strips the per-window statistics the router needs; raw pre-normalization routing fixes it

321   not sharp on shape-coupled data (R=0.06 Solar); forecasting and imputation only.

\section{Information Content Diagnostic}
\label{app:diagnostic}

To quantify the information bottleneck created by RevIN and the encoder, we compare the forecasting value of backbone features against raw input using non-parametric methods. All experiments run on CPU with zero GPU cost.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{\textbf{Backbone features vs.\ raw input} (H=96, 80/20 train/test split). k-NN: k-nearest neighbor regression ($k{=}20$, distance-weighted) on mean-pooled backbone features vs.\ raw 512-dim input. Ridge: regularized linear regression (cross-validated $\alpha$). Backbone features are $16$--$33\%$ worse than raw input even with a universal approximator (k-NN), confirming genuine information destruction.}
\label{tab:diagnostic}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}
\toprule
& \multicolumn{2}{c}{k-NN (MSE)} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Ridge (MSE)} & Backbone \\
\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5}
Dataset & Raw input & Backbone & Raw input & Backbone & penalty \\
\midrule
ETTh1   & $0.636$ & $0.737$ & $0.551$ & $1.230$ & $+16\%$ \\
ETTm1   & $0.498$ & $0.614$ & $0.363$ & $0.708$ & $+23\%$ \\
Weather & $0.453$ & $0.601$ & $0.300$ & $0.661$ & $+33\%$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Residual correction experiments further confirm that backbone features provide negligible complementary signal: adding backbone features (via Ridge or Random Forest) as a residual correction atop a linear raw-input predictor improves MSE by at most $0.55\%$. On ETTh1, Random Forest regression on raw input alone reduces MSE by $44\%$ versus Ridge, revealing substantial nonlinear temporal structure in the raw data. However, Random Forest on backbone features adds only $+0.02\%$ to the linear baseline, indicating that nearly all of this nonlinear structure is lost in the encoder. Few-shot experiments (Table~\ref{tab:fewshot}) show the pattern holds at every tested training set size $N \in \{10, \ldots, 5000\}$: DLinear dominates at every such $N$, indicating data scarcity is not a regime in which the frozen backbone recovers its advantage within the range tested. The gap to DLinear is consistent with information destruction by the normalization--encoding pipeline rather than a limitation of adapter design or training data volume.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{\textbf{Few-shot learning curve} (H=96, mean$\pm$std over seeds $\{42,43,44\}$). DLinear (49K params, trained from scratch) dominates frozen RR-MoA-full (426K params) at every tested $N \in \{10,\ldots,5000\}$, confirming the gap reflects encoder information loss rather than data efficiency.}
\label{tab:fewshot}
\scriptsize
\begin{tabular}{@{}rcccccc@{}}
\toprule
& \multicolumn{2}{c}{ETTh1} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Weather} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Electricity} \\
\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5} \cmidrule(lr){6-7}
$N$ & DLinear & RR-MoA & DLinear & RR-MoA & DLinear & RR-MoA \\
\midrule
10   & $\mathit{0.859}$ & $1.530$ & $\mathit{0.361}$ & $1.265$ & $\mathit{0.601}$ & $1.384$ \\
100  & $\mathit{0.630}$ & $1.172$ & $\mathit{0.286}$ & $0.773$ & $\mathit{0.373}$ & $1.103$ \\
1000 & $\mathit{0.453}$ & $0.816$ & $\mathit{0.213}$ & $0.378$ & $\mathit{0.188}$ & $0.732$ \\
5000 & $\mathit{0.421}$ & $0.692$ & $\mathit{0.205}$ & $0.288$ & $\mathit{0.158}$ & $0.396$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\section{Cross-Backbone RR-MoA}

\end{observation}

In the corner case where routing signal lives entirely in the stripped statistics ($E$ deterministic in $(M,\Sigma)$, $(M,\Sigma)\perp\!\!\!\perp S$; Part~(iii), Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}), $I(X;E){-}I(S;E)=H(E)$: RevIN destroys the entire routing distribution. We use the \emph{signal ratio} $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\mathrm{Var}(M)+\mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)]/\overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ as a tractable monotone proxy. The bound is empirically tight (CCA $\varepsilon\in[0.09,1.26]$ nats, KSG-validated~\citep{kraskov2004ksg}; gap $0.075$ nats, Appendix~\ref{app:mi_tightness}); learnable $\alpha$ converges to $\alpha{<}0.5$ on $30/30$ runs (Appendix~\ref{app:learnable_alpha}). $R(\mathcal{D})$ predicts raw-routing benefit at Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$ across the 6 LTSF benchmarks plus Exchange, Traffic, Solar; Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}), whereas $H(E\mid S)$ alone does not ($\rho{=}{-}0.12$). Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, $\Delta{=}{+}2.9\%$) is correctly flagged a boundary case; Solar ($R{=}0.06$) is the one outlier (SSR $+43\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}).

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\vspace{-12pt}
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.58\columnwidth}
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[
---
\end{minipage}
\vspace{-8pt}
\caption{\textbf{Left: Frozen Paradox}, frozen RR-MoA beats full FT by $12$--$79\%$. \textbf{Right: Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} validated} ($\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$). Traffic and Solar are boundary cases.}
\label{fig:frozen_paradox}
\label{fig:signal_ratio}
\vspace{-10pt}
\end{figure}

{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Cross-backbone, task, horizon.}\label{sec:cross_backbone}\label{sec:topk} RR-MoA wins on every cross-backbone cell for instance-normalized backbones (MOMENT-small/large: $26$--$79\%$/$32$--$75\%$; Moirai: $12$--$29\%$; Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe}: $37$--$90\%$; Tables~\ref{tab:rrmoa},~\ref{tab:rrmoa_extended},~\ref{tab:cross_backbone},~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}), and predicted negative controls (Chronos, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, no instance norm) show no collapse (Table~\ref{tab:timer_xl}). Top-2 routing (Appendix~\ref{app:topk}), imputation $7/8$ (Appendix~\ref{app:imputation}), all $24/24$ horizons (Table~\ref{tab:horizon}), and Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+} on 6 datasets $\times$ 5 backbones $\times$ 4 horizons (Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}) confirm robustness.}

\vspace{-0.6em}
\section{Conclusion and Limitations}
\vspace{-0.6em}
