                                           I(X; E) − I(S; E) ≥ I(M, Σ; E) − I(M, Σ; S),
299               where ε := I(M, Σ; S) ≥ 0 captures any residual “leakage” of the stripped statistics
300               through the shape. Equality holds iff (M, Σ) ⊥⊥ S | E.

301   In the corner case where routing signal lives entirely in the stripped statistics (E deterministic in
302   (M, Σ), (M, Σ) ⊥    ⊥ S; Part (iii), Appendix D), I(X; E)−I(S; E) = H(E): RevIN destroys the
303   entire routing distribution. We use the signal ratio R(D) = [Var(M )+Var(Σ)]/Var(S) as a tractable
304   monotone proxy. The bound is empirically tight (CCA ε ∈ [0.09, 1.26] nats, KSG-validated [25];
305   gap 0.075 nats, Appendix L); learnable α converges to α<0.5 on 30/30 runs (Appendix K). R(D)
306   predicts raw-routing benefit at Spearman ρ=−0.88 (p<0.002, n=9 across the 6 LTSF benchmarks
307   plus Exchange, Traffic, Solar; Figure M.1), whereas H(E | S) alone does not (ρ=−0.12). Traffic

316   5   Conclusion and Limitations
317   MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail because the normalization that handles distribution
318   shift also strips the per-window statistics the router needs; raw pre-normalization routing fixes it
319   (and motivates Residual-IA+ ), with R(D) identifying where it applies, including a vision-modality
320   replication (Appendix H.1). Limits: instance-level normalization only; R(D) is a monotone proxy,
321   not sharp on shape-coupled data (R=0.06 Solar); forecasting and imputation only.

322   References

310   Cross-backbone, task, horizon. RR-MoA wins on every cell for instance-normalized backbones

315   5   Conclusion and Limitations
316   MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail because the normalization that handles distribution
317   shift also strips the per-window statistics the router needs; raw pre-normalization routing fixes it
318   (and motivates Residual-IA+ ), with R(D) identifying where it applies, including a vision-modality
319   replication (Appendix H.1). Limits: instance-level normalization only; R(D) is a monotone proxy,
320   not sharp on shape-coupled data (R=0.06 Solar); forecasting and imputation only.

274   Closing the DLinear gap. DLinear (49K params, from scratch) outperforms frozen adapters in
275   absolute MSE (Table 4, grey rows): k-NN/Ridge diagnostics on backbone vs. raw features are
276   consistent with the gap reflecting information destruction by the normalization-encoding pipeline
277   rather than insufficient adapter capacity (Appendix M). Restoring raw-signal access at the expert
278   level closes it: Residual-IA+ (shared NLinear raw branch + per-expert gated backbone residual;
279   Appendix P.1) matches or beats DLinear on 6/6 datasets at H=96 (up to −7.5%) and 107/123 cells
280   across six backbones; a self-routed SR-RIA+ (Appendix P.2) hits the same 6/6 with 3 outright wins,
281   and a TSFM-free Pure Raw-MLP MoE control (Appendix P.3) localizes the TSFM’s contribution.
282   Rawness vs. bypass and dose-response. Replacing the router’s raw input with RevIN-normalized
283   input degrades MSE by 60–88% while entropy stays high (Table F.1): the router relies on the content
284   of the stripped statistics, not the bypass path. A dose-response interpolation xα = (1−α)xraw +
285   α RevIN(xraw ) reveals a phase transition (Figure D.1; 150 runs): MSE degrades by +5.4% for
286   α≤0.75 but jumps to +283% at α=1, with entropy held in [1.43, 1.57] throughout. The heuristic
287   SNR-onset threshold is at α∗ = R(D)/(R(D) + 1) ≈ 0.40 for the median benchmark (Appendix D.1
288   labels Proposition 2 as a heuristic prediction); a temporal-shuffle ablation indicates routing depends
289   on (M, Σ) rather than temporal ordering (Appendix F).
290   Why RevIN destroys routing: formal analysis. Each window xi decomposes into mean Mi =
291   µ(xi ), scale Σi = σ(xi ), and shape Si = (xi − Mi )/Σi ; RevIN maps xi 7→ Si and discards
292   (Mi , Σi ). The mutual-information chain rule quantifies how much routing information lives in the
293   discarded part.
294   Observation 1 (Routing Information Loss Under Instance Normalization). Let X be a random
295   input window, E ∈ {1, . . . , K} the expert assignment produced by any deterministic router, and
296   S = (X − M )/Σ the RevIN-normalized shape.
297           (i) Exact decomposition. I(X; E) − I(S; E) = I(M, Σ; E | S) ≥ 0.
298        (ii) Quantitative bound. Without any independence assumption,
                                           I(X; E) − I(S; E) ≥ I(M, Σ; E) − I(M, Σ; S),
299               where ε := I(M, Σ; S) ≥ 0 captures any residual “leakage” of the stripped statistics
300               through the shape. Equality holds iff (M, Σ) ⊥⊥ S | E.
301   In the corner case where routing signal lives entirely in the stripped statistics (E deterministic in
302   (M, Σ), (M, Σ) ⊥    ⊥ S; Part (iii), Appendix D), I(X; E)−I(S; E) = H(E): RevIN destroys the
303   entire routing distribution. We use the signal ratio R(D) = [Var(M )+Var(Σ)]/Var(S) as a tractable
304   monotone proxy. The bound is empirically tight (CCA ε ∈ [0.09, 1.26] nats, KSG-validated [25];
305   gap 0.075 nats, Appendix L); learnable α converges to α<0.5 on 30/30 runs (Appendix K). R(D)
306   predicts raw-routing benefit at Spearman ρ=−0.88 (p<0.002, n=9; Figure M.1), whereas H(E | S)
307   alone does not (ρ=−0.12); Traffic (R=0.14) is correctly flagged a boundary, Solar (R=0.06) the
308   one outlier (Appendix F).

552:\textbf{Closing the DLinear gap.} DLinear (49K params, from scratch) outperforms frozen adapters in absolute MSE (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, grey rows): k-NN/Ridge diagnostics on backbone vs.\ raw features are consistent with the gap reflecting information destruction by the normalization-encoding pipeline rather than insufficient adapter capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:diagnostic}). Restoring raw-signal access \emph{at the expert level} closes it: \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (shared NLinear raw branch $+$ per-expert gated backbone residual; Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}) matches or beats DLinear on $6/6$ datasets at $H{=}96$ (up to $-7.5\%$) and $107/123$ cells across six backbones; a self-routed SR-RIA\textsuperscript{+} (Appendix~\ref{app:sr_ria}) hits the same $6/6$ with $3$ outright wins, and a TSFM-free Pure Raw-MLP MoE control (Appendix~\ref{app:raw_mlp_moe}) localizes the TSFM's contribution.
554:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Rawness vs.\ bypass and dose-response.} Replacing the router's raw input with RevIN-normalized input degrades MSE by $60$--$88\%$ while entropy stays high (Table~\ref{tab:router_input}): the router relies on the \emph{content} of the stripped statistics, not the bypass path. A dose-response interpolation $\mathbf{x}_\alpha = (1{-}\alpha)\mathbf{x}_\text{raw} + \alpha\,\text{RevIN}(\mathbf{x}_\text{raw})$ reveals a phase transition (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}; $150$ runs): MSE degrades by $+5.4\%$ for $\alpha{\leq}0.75$ but jumps to $+283\%$ at $\alpha{=}1$, with entropy held in $[1.43, 1.57]$ throughout. The heuristic SNR-onset threshold is at $\alpha^* = R(\mathcal{D})/(R(\mathcal{D})+1) \approx 0.40$ for the median benchmark (Appendix~\ref{app:prop2_depth} labels Proposition~\ref{prop:phase_transition} as a heuristic prediction); a temporal-shuffle ablation indicates routing depends on $(M,\Sigma)$ rather than temporal ordering (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}).}
557:\textbf{Why RevIN destroys routing: formal analysis.} Each window $\mathbf{x}_i$ decomposes into mean $M_i = \mu(\mathbf{x}_i)$, scale $\Sigma_i = \sigma(\mathbf{x}_i)$, and shape $\mathbf{S}_i = (\mathbf{x}_i - M_i)/\Sigma_i$; RevIN maps $\mathbf{x}_i \mapsto \mathbf{S}_i$ and discards $(M_i, \Sigma_i)$. The mutual-information chain rule quantifies how much routing information lives in the discarded part.
1245:\caption{\textbf{Rawness vs.\ bypass} (frozen, Top-2, 3 seeds). RevIN router reads the same input after per-window normalization. Stripping statistics degrades MSE by 60--88\%.}
2150:\caption{\textbf{Closing the DLinear gap} (MOMENT-small, strictly frozen, Top-2, 3 seeds, H=96). Dual-stream experts that access both hidden states and raw input narrow the gap from $+33$--$75\%$ to $+9$--$11\%$ and \textbf{beat DLinear on Weather}. Learned $\alpha{\approx}0.49$ confirms the backbone contributes complementary information when combined with raw access.}
```

311   5   Conclusion and Limitations
312   MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail because the normalization that handles distribution
313   shift also strips the per-window statistics the router needs; raw pre-normalization routing fixes it,
314   motivates Residual-IA+ , and R(D) identifies where it applies (vision replication, Appendix H.1).
315   Limits: instance-level only; R(D) monotone, not sharp on shape-coupled data; forecasting +
316   imputation only.

```
\textbf{Why RevIN destroys routing.} Each window decomposes into mean $M_i$, scale $\Sigma_i$, shape $\mathbf{S}_i = (\mathbf{x}_i - M_i)/\Sigma_i$; RevIN keeps $\mathbf{S}_i$ and discards $(M_i,\Sigma_i)$. The chain rule quantifies how much routing signal lives in the discarded part.

\begin{observation}[Routing Information Loss Under Instance Normalization]
\label{thm:mi_decomp}
Let $X$ be a random input window, $E \in \{1,\ldots,K\}$ the expert assignment produced by any deterministic router, and $S = (X - M)/\Sigma$ the RevIN-normalized shape.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item[\textup{(i)}] \textbf{Exact decomposition.} $I(X; E) - I(S; E) = I(M, \Sigma; E \mid S) \geq 0$.
    \item[\textup{(ii)}] \textbf{Quantitative bound.} Without any independence assumption,
    \[
        I(X; E) - I(S; E) \;\geq\; I(M, \Sigma;\, E) \;-\; I(M, \Sigma;\, S),
    \]
    where $\varepsilon := I(M, \Sigma;\, S) \geq 0$ captures any residual ``leakage'' of the stripped statistics through the shape. Equality holds iff $(M,\Sigma) \perp\!\!\!\perp S \mid E$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{observation}

In the corner case where routing signal lives entirely in the stripped statistics ($E$ deterministic in $(M,\Sigma)$, $(M,\Sigma)\perp\!\!\!\perp S$; Part~(iii), Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}), $I(X;E){-}I(S;E)=H(E)$: RevIN destroys the entire routing distribution. We use the \emph{signal ratio} $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\mathrm{Var}(M)+\mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)]/\overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ as a tractable monotone proxy. The bound is empirically tight (CCA $\varepsilon\in[0.09,1.26]$ nats, KSG-validated~\citep{kraskov2004ksg}; gap $0.075$ nats, Appendix~\ref{app:mi_tightness}); learnable $\alpha$ converges to $\alpha{<}0.5$ on $30/30$ runs (Appendix~\ref{app:learnable_alpha}). $R(\mathcal{D})$ predicts raw-routing benefit at Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$; Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}), whereas $H(E\mid S)$ alone does not ($\rho{=}{-}0.12$); Traffic ($R{=}0.14$) is correctly flagged a boundary, Solar ($R{=}0.06$) the one outlier (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}).

{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Cross-backbone, task, horizon.}\label{sec:cross_backbone}\label{sec:topk} RR-MoA wins on every cell for instance-normalized backbones (MOMENT-small/large $26$--$79\%/32$--$75\%$, Moirai $12$--$29\%$, Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} $37$--$90\%$); LayerNorm-only controls (Chronos, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}) show no collapse. Top-$2$, imputation $7/8$, $24/24$ horizons, and Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+} ($6{\times}5{\times}4$) confirm robustness (Tables~\ref{tab:cross_backbone}--\ref{tab:horizon}; Appendices~\ref{app:topk},~\ref{app:imputation},~\ref{app:gate_pathology}).}
```

10:\item {\bf Limitations}
11:    \item[] Question: Does the paper discuss the limitations of the work performed by the authors?
12-    \item[] Answer: \answerYes{}
13:    \item[] Justification: The Limitations paragraph in Section~5 discusses three limitations: (i) \emph{normalization specificity} --- the diagnosis covers instance-level normalization (RevIN, BatchNorm, GroupNorm), and extensions to attention-based or learned normalizers are not characterized; (ii) \emph{signal-ratio boundary cases} --- the predictor $R(\mathcal{D})$ is a single monotone proxy for variance contribution, with Solar ($R{=}0.06$) flagged but not sharp; (iii) \emph{forecasting and imputation only} --- anomaly detection, classification, and irregularly-sampled tasks are deferred. Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, $\Delta{=}{+}2.9\%$) is honestly reported as a boundary case where RR-MoA correctly does not improve.
14-
15-\item {\bf Theory assumptions and proofs}
16-    \item[] Question: For each theoretical result, does the paper provide the full set of assumptions and a complete (and correct) proof?
17-    \item[] Answer: \answerYes{}
18-    \item[] Justification: The paper has three theoretical results, each with explicit assumptions and complete derivations in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}. Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} (Routing Information Loss Under Instance Normalization) Parts~(i)--(ii) hold \emph{without any independence assumption}; Part~(iii) (full-information corner) additionally assumes $(M,\Sigma)\perp S$ (RevIN's design assumption) and $E\perp S\mid(M,\Sigma)$ (empirically validated via the per-sample expert assignment scatter, Figure~\ref{fig:routing_viz}). The bound is also measured directly via CCA, KSG, and held-out classifier (Appendix~\ref{app:mi_tightness}), with the gap averaging $0.075$ nats. Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen} (Gradient Co-Adaptation) includes its full assumptions and proof in the same appendix. Proposition~\ref{prop:phase_transition} (Heuristic SNR Onset and Softmax Discontinuity) is explicitly labeled heuristic, with its derivation given inline and its prediction validated empirically by the 150-run dose-response grid (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}). Derivations rely on the data processing inequality, the chain rule of mutual information, standard softmax gradient calculus, and linear-variance scaling.

2:% Use \usepackage[final]{neurips_2026} for camera-ready
58:Time series foundation models pair a frozen pretrained backbone with a task-specific head. Because downstream series mix heterogeneous regimes, the natural upgrade is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) head: a router over architecturally distinct experts, one per window. However, this upgrade fails on instance-normalized backbones: Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN) and similar layers strip the per-window mean and variance the router needs, a failure we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}. Whenever joint training makes even one backbone transformer block trainable, routing entropy falls to $0.000$, meaning that every input is sent to a single expert and the rest are never activated. A $720$-run sweep across standard MoE rescue mechanisms recovers at most $10.9\%$ MSE ($2.7\times$ worse than our causal intervention), because the failure is in the router's input, not its optimization. We formalize this via a mutual-information decomposition; the resulting signal-ratio statistic, computable before training, predicts dataset vulnerability and flags boundary cases. The diagnosis prescribes a minimal causal intervention: \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA sets a new state of the art for adapter-based foundation-model transfer under a strictly frozen backbone: $54/54$ wins on the primary backbone ($26$--$79\%$ MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter), strictly beats LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning at $p{<}0.001$, and generalizes across four further backbones and an imputation task. Causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and reveal a \emph{Frozen Paradox}: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$. Applying the same raw-signal principle at the expert level (Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}) closes the gap with the strong DLinear baseline across six backbones and four horizons.
70:The failure is specific to learnable-affine instance normalization placed inside the encoder pipeline. Backbones such as Moirai, which apply only non-learnable per-instance I/O scaling and use RMSNorm internally (rather than RevIN-style learnable-affine normalization upstream of the encoder), do not collapse (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}), and standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, entropy regularization, z-loss, ReLU routing, expert-choice) recover at most $10.9\%$ MSE, still $2.7\times$ worse than RR-MoA (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}), because the problem lies in the router's \emph{input}, not its optimization.
78:\item We propose RR-MoA, the \textbf{minimal causal intervention}: changing only the router's input from normalized hidden states to $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$, with no change to the optimizer, the experts, or the frozen backbone. RR-MoA wins $54/54$ cells against AdaMix (the same architecture with collapsed routing), strictly dominates $11$ optimization-side rescue mechanisms ($720$ runs; best rescue recovers $\leq 10.9\%$, RR-MoA $2.7{\times}$ better), and is not subsumed by naive $(\mu,\sigma)$ re-injection ($+42$--$239\%$ MSE; $11/18$ cells re-collapse). Two architecturally distinct variants confirm the principle generalizes beyond RR-MoA's specific router: \textbf{SR-MoA} (self-routed, no external router; outperforms RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets) and \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (expert-level raw-input dual-stream; matches or beats DLinear on $6/6$ datasets, generalizing to $107/123$ cells across six backbones).

34:\newcommand{\nonterminal}[1]{\texttt{#1}}

1481:\textbf{Router-Detached Gradient Flow (RDGF).} A variant that unfreezes the backbone but replaces routing-weighted gradients with uniform-average gradients ($\nabla_H \mathcal{L}_\text{backbone} = \frac{1}{K}\sum_k \nabla_H \mathcal{L}_k$) degrades MSE by $+35\%$ to $+100\%$ across the three datasets we tested (ETTh1, ETTm1, Weather; frozen MOMENT-small last-2, 3 seeds; per-run JSONs in \texttt{evidence\_vm/rdgf/}). This is consistent with the Frozen Paradox being structural rather than an artifact of full-gradient unfreezing.
1483:\textbf{Spectral-temporal router (FFT-Router).} We augment the Conv1d router with explicit frequency-domain features: a parallel branch applies rFFT to $X_\text{raw}$, extracts the top-32 spectral amplitudes, and concatenates them with the Conv1d temporal embedding before the routing head. On the two datasets with matched dense-frozen baselines (Weather, ETTm1; frozen MOMENT-small, 3 seeds; per-run JSONs in \texttt{evidence\_vm/fft\_router/}), FFT-Router \emph{degrades} MSE by $+6\%$ (Weather) and $+22\%$ (ETTm1). The Conv1d router appears to already capture frequency-relevant patterns implicitly; adding explicit spectral features overparameterizes the router without improving routing quality on these two datasets.
2037:\textbf{Independent ensemble on Timer-XL.} On MOMENT-small, the independent ensemble (5 experts trained separately, averaged at inference) is $37$--$46\%$ worse than RR-MoA, indicating that learned routing adds value beyond diversity. On Timer-XL the pattern \emph{reverses} on the three forecasting datasets we extended (ETTh1, ETTm1, ETTm2; per-run JSONs in \texttt{evidence\_vm/timer\_xl\_ensemble/}): the independent ensemble has lower MSE than both AdaMix and RR-MoA (ETTh1 $0.462$, ETTm1 $0.384$, ETTm2 $0.296$ vs AdaMix $0.477$, $0.399$, $0.326$). When hidden-state routing is healthy (no RevIN), simple averaging is a strong baseline that learned routing does not consistently beat. This is consistent with RR-MoA's advantage being specifically about rescuing collapsed routing under RevIN.
2339:\caption{\textbf{Gate-pathology diagnostic.} Mean per-sample gate activation $g_k(\mathbf{X})$ averaged across experts, seeds (3), and test windows for each architecture--training-recipe combination. Across the three rows, all six datasets converge to $g \approx 0.5$, indicating the gate provides no per-window discriminative signal at 15 epochs (per-run JSONs in \texttt{evidence\_vm/gate\_diagnostic/}).}
2411:\item \textbf{H1 (data scarcity).} ETTh1/ETTh2 have $8{,}640$ raw samples each versus $34{,}560$ for ETTm*, and \texttt{max\_samples$=$5000} caps the adapter training set at $\approx 9\%$ of available windows for ETTh but $\approx 2\%$ for ETTm. The ETTh pair may be overly exposed to sampling noise.
2415:The two hypotheses make opposite interventions: H1 predicts that \emph{more data} (raising \texttt{max\_samples} to $100{,}000$) closes the gap; H2 predicts that \emph{fewer parameters} (sharing the raw branch across experts to reach DLinear's $49$K budget) closes it. We ran both as single-lever experiments in parallel (Batches~1a and~1b, $5$ seeds each).
2418:\emph{Cap removal fails.} At \texttt{max\_samples$=$100000}, the ETTh2 gap widens from $+10.6\%$ to $+26\%$. The additional training data at the same $15$-epoch, lr${=}10^{-3}$, wd${=}10^{-4}$ recipe produces $11{\times}$ more update steps, over-regularizing through weight decay and preventing cosine-schedule convergence. This rules out H1 under the original training recipe; an extended-schedule retest (cosine + weight-decay sweep over the larger sample budget) would be needed to rule it out under all training recipes.
2424:\emph{Non-stationarity (Batch~4).} Hourly ETT data exhibits pronounced per-window level drift: the train/test windows have different means. Standard LTSF practice addresses this with NLinear: $\mathbf{y} = \text{Linear}(\mathbf{x} - x_T) + x_T$, where $x_T$ is a gradient-free anchor (the last input value). The linear layer is thereby constrained to model \emph{relative motion}, not absolute level. Testing \texttt{--raw-arch nlinear} as a drop-in replacement reduces ETTh1 from $+5.1\%$ to $+3.3\%$ and ETTh2 from $+10.6\%$ to $+6.9\%$ at $n{=}3$ seeds, while \emph{improving} (not regressing) ETTm2 ($-1.6\% \to -4.1\%$) and Weather ($-6.6\% \to -8.7\%$). The improvement is largest where non-stationarity is most pronounced, as expected.
2425:\emph{Per-dataset epoch mismatch (Batch~2).} A fixed $15$-epoch schedule assumes all datasets converge at the same rate. We test this by running validation-based early stopping with patience $5$ and logging the best epoch per seed (per-run logs in \texttt{evidence\_vm/early\_stopping/}). The result is striking: ETTh1 converges at epoch $7.2{\pm}1.2$, ETTh2 at $8.6{\pm}2.8$, ETTm1 at $14.8{\pm}3.7$, Weather at $8.6{\pm}1.2$, Electricity at $20.2{\pm}4.0$. No single fixed budget fits all six datasets: the $15$-epoch schedule simultaneously over-fits the ETTh pair and under-fits Electricity by $5$--$10$ epochs. Val-based early stopping (with gradient clipping at $\lVert g \rVert_2 \leq 1.0$ to stabilize the first epoch) achieves dataset-adaptive budgets without per-dataset hyperparameter tuning. In isolation this drops Electricity from $+0.6\%$ to $0.0\%$ (perfect parity, $p{=}0.991$) and Weather from $-6.6\%$ to $-4.6\%$ (nearing significance).
```

71:## 5. Paper number ↔ JSON file traceability
---FORENSIC tail:---
See `manifest_md5.txt` — MD5 hash of every log file and every JSON file in
this directory. Any modification to any file will change its hash. The logs
and JSONs here are bit-for-bit identical to the ones currently on the VM at
`~/neuralips26/results/{rr_moa,adamix}/`.

## 8. How to reproduce from scratch

On an A10G machine with the repo checked out to commit `337ffa3` or later:

```bash
# One-shot: runs all 66 experiments used in the paper
nohup python3 scripts/run_freeze_ablation.py --experiment all --device cuda \
  --seed 42 --epochs 15 > results/freeze_ablation_run.log 2>&1 &

# Multi-seed sweep (used for Tables 3–5): runs seeds {42,43,44} end-to-end
bash scripts/run_multiseed.sh        # ~2.5 hours on a single A10G
```

Each invocation of `run_rr_moa.py` / `run_adamix.py` is idempotent and writes
a single self-contained JSON file under `results/rr_moa/` or `results/adamix/`
whose name encodes the dataset, horizon, K, sparsity, freeze-level, and seed.
Every JSON contains the full hyperparameter state, the seed, the backbone
trainable parameter count, the final MSE/MAE, the routing weight vector, and
the Shannon entropy of the routing distribution. There is no hidden state
between runs.

---

_Evidence retrieved and audited on 2026-04-05 at the request of the author
in response to a reviewer challenge alleging result fabrication._

## Audit summary — new findings (rounds 3-4)

### CRITICAL — supplementary archive identity leaks

The `evidence_vm/` directory contains **three files that would desk-reject the submission if uploaded as-is**:

**`evidence_vm/FORENSIC_AUDIT.md`** — author identity:
- `SSH key | hungphanphd.pem (RACE VM credentials)` — your literal name + RMIT VM identifier
- `VM IP | ec2-13-238-161-176.ap-southeast-2.compute.amazonaws.com` — public AWS hostname
- `Host | ip-10-110-161-2 (AWS, ap-southeast-2)` — AWS region
- `~/neuralips26/results/{rr_moa,adamix}/` — local repo path
- Footer: "*at the request of the author in response to a reviewer challenge alleging result fabrication.*" — implies prior reviewed submission, also uses non-anonymous "the author"

**`evidence_vm/system_metadata.txt`** — host/AWS metadata:

## What you upload at the NeurIPS 2026 deadline

There are **two separate uploads** through OpenReview, with two different scopes:

### 1. Paper PDF (mandatory, at the deadline)
- The `main.pdf` (anonymized, 9-page main body + references + appendix + checklist).
- The OpenReview metadata: title, abstract, TL;DR, subject area, anonymized author list.
- That's it. **Nothing else is required at the deadline itself.**

### 2. Supplementary material (optional; separate uploader; often a separate deadline)
- A single `.zip` archive that can contain code, raw results, extended appendix, video, etc.
- In recent NeurIPS years (2023–2025) the supplementary deadline has been either *same as paper* or *up to 1 week later* — varies by year. **You should confirm the NeurIPS 2026 supplementary cutoff on the call-for-papers page** (I don't have it memorized; the supervisor or the NeurIPS website is the authority).
- Reviewers can read it during review but are not required to. Most don't.
- If you upload it, **it must also be anonymized** — same double-blind rules apply.

### So what does this mean for `evidence_vm/`?

**The desk-reject risk from `evidence_vm/FORENSIC_AUDIT.md` only triggers if you upload supplementary material that contains it.** It does *not* affect the paper PDF itself.

You have three options:

| Option | What you do | Cost | Risk |
|---|---|---|---|
| **A. Don't upload supplementary at all** | Paper-PDF-only submission. Update `checklist.tex` to remove "supplementary material" claims (currently 2 places say it exists). | 5-min checklist edit. | Reviewers can't run `verify.py`; checklist Q on "code released" goes from "yes" to "upon acceptance". Soft credibility hit, no desk-reject. |
| **B. Upload `evidence_vm/` after scrubbing** | Delete `FORENSIC_AUDIT.md` + `system_metadata.txt` + `vm_file_listing.txt` + `.DS_Store`; zip the rest. Submit. | 10–15 min before whichever deadline applies. | Zero, if scrub is thorough. |
| **C. Upload at supplementary deadline (delayed)** | Ship paper at May deadline, scrub `evidence_vm/` over the next week, upload supplementary later. | Buys you time. Same scrub work needed. | Same as B if scrubbed; if you forget = desk-reject risk. |

### Checklist alignment problem

Right now `checklist.tex` says (line 28 and 68) that supplementary material is included:

> "all code, raw JSON result files, and a self-verification script (verify.py) … are included in the supplementary material"

> "The supplementary material includes all code, a README with reproduction instructions, and raw JSON result files. The code will be released under MIT license upon acceptance."

If you go Option A (no supplementary), these statements become false → soft AC concern (not desk-reject, but reviewers cross-check). You'd want to soften them to something like "code and raw JSON results will be released upon acceptance under MIT license."

### My recommendation

**Option B**: scrub `evidence_vm/` now while the changes are still small, then upload it with the paper. Reasons:
1. The scrub is ~15 min of work and reversible.
2. The checklist claim "all numbers traceable to JSONs" is one of the paper's reviewer-trust differentiators; uploading the JSONs lets that claim land.
3. NeurIPS reviewers reward visible reproducibility infrastructure even if they don't run it.

**Decision needed from you**:
1. Pick A, B, or C.
2. If B or C: am I authorized to delete `FORENSIC_AUDIT.md`, `system_metadata.txt`, `vm_file_listing.txt`, `.DS_Store`? (I will keep a copy of `FORENSIC_AUDIT.md` outside the repo if you want a personal record.)
3. The 5 missing-subdir paper references — keep, replace with generic phrasing, or fill with real JSONs from `results/`?

Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} and Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe}: model \emph{weights} are released under CC-BY-NC-4.0 (research/non-commercial use only) on \texttt{Salesforce/moirai-1.1-R-small} and \texttt{Salesforce/moirai-moe-1.0-R-small}; the supporting \texttt{uni2ts} code is Apache 2.0. Our use is academic research, consistent with the non-commercial weight license. Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}: Apache 2.0, \texttt{amazon/chronos-t5-small}. Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}: Apache 2.0, \texttt{thuml/timer-base-84m}. \textbf{Datasets.} ETT (ETTh1/h2/m1/m2) is introduced by~\citet{zhou2021informer} and released under CC-BY-ND-4.0 (NoDerivatives); we ship only loader scripts and do not redistribute preprocessed arrays. Weather is the MPI-BGC Jena meteorological recording, redistributed for the LTSF benchmark by Wu et al.~\citep{wu2021autoformer}. Electricity is the UCI \texttt{ElectricityLoadDiagrams20112014} dataset (CC-BY-4.0, originally collected by Artur Trindade). Traffic (Caltrans PeMS), Solar (NREL Solar Power Data), and Exchange (multi-currency exchange rates) were curated for time series benchmarking by Lai et al.~\citep{lai2018lstnet}; the source data is public-record, but the curated benchmark CSVs do not ship with an explicit redistribution license, so we again only ship loader scripts pointing to the upstream sources. The Monash time series forecasting archive~\citep{godahewa2021monash} (CC-BY-4.0) provides standardized versions of several of these datasets and is the recommended reproducibility anchor. No dataset is used outside the standardized LTSF forecasting and imputation tasks for which it was collected.

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/main.tex",
  "old_string": "Time series foundation models pair a frozen pretrained backbone with a task-specific head. Because downstream series mix heterogeneous regimes, the natural upgrade is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) head: a router over architecturally distinct experts, one per window. However, this upgrade fails on instance-normalized backbones: Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN) and similar layers strip the per-window mean and variance the router needs, a failure we call \\emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}. Whenever joint training makes even one backbone transformer block trainable, routing entropy falls to $0.000$, meaning that every input is sent to a single expert and the rest are never activated. A $720$-run sweep across standard MoE rescue mechanisms recovers at most $10.9\\%$ MSE ($2.7\\times$ worse than our causal intervention), because the failure is in the router's input, not its optimization. We formalize this via a mutual-information decomposition; the resulting signal-ratio statistic, computable before training, predicts dataset vulnerability and flags boundary cases. The diagnosis prescribes a minimal causal intervention: \\emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA sets a new state of the art for adapter-based foundation-model transfer under a strictly frozen backbone: $54/54$ wins on the primary backbone ($26$--$79\\%$ MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter), strictly beats LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning at $p{<}0.001$, and generalizes across four further backbones and an imputation task. Causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and reveal a \\emph{Frozen Paradox}: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\\%$. Applying the same raw-signal principle at the expert level (Residual-IA\\textsuperscript{+}) closes the gap with the strong DLinear baseline across six backbones and four horizons.",
  "new_string": "Time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT pair a frozen backbone with a task-specific head. Because downstream series mix heterogeneous regimes, the natural upgrade is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) head: a router over architecturally distinct experts, one per window. However, this upgrade fails on instance-normalized backbones: Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN) and similar layers strip the per-window mean and variance the router needs, a failure we call \\emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}. Whenever joint training makes even one backbone transformer block trainable, routing entropy falls to $0.000$, meaning that every input is sent to a single expert and the rest are never activated. A $720$-run sweep across standard MoE rescue mechanisms recovers at most $10.9\\%$ MSE ($2.7\\times$ worse than our causal intervention), because the failure is in the router's input, not its optimization. We formalize this via a mutual-information decomposition; the resulting signal-ratio statistic, computable before training, predicts dataset vulnerability (Spearman $\\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$) and flags boundary cases. The diagnosis prescribes a minimal causal intervention: \\emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA sets a new state of the art for adapter-based TSFM transfer under a strictly frozen backbone: $54/54$ wins on the primary backbone ($26$--$79\\%$ MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter), strictly beats LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning at $p{<}0.001$, and generalizes across four further backbones and an imputation task. Eight causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and reveal a \\emph{Frozen Paradox}: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\\%$. Applying the same raw-signal principle at the expert level (Residual-IA\\textsuperscript{+}) closes the gap with DLinear on $107/123$ cells across six backbones $\\times$ four horizons."

57	\begin{abstract}
58	Pioneering time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT, TimesFM, and TTM pair a frozen backbone with a task-specific head. Because downstream series mix heterogeneous regimes, the natural upgrade is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) head: a router over architecturally distinct experts, one per window. However, this upgrade fails on instance-normalized backbones: Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN) and similar layers strip the per-window mean and variance the router needs, a failure we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}. Whenever joint training makes even one backbone transformer block trainable, routing entropy falls to $0.000$, meaning that every input is sent to a single expert and the rest are never activated. A $720$-run sweep across standard MoE rescue mechanisms recovers at most $10.9\%$ MSE ($2.7\times$ worse than our causal intervention), because the failure is in the router's input, not its optimization. We formalize this via a mutual-information decomposition; the resulting signal-ratio statistic, computable before training, predicts dataset vulnerability (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$) and flags boundary cases. The diagnosis prescribes a minimal causal intervention: \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA sets a new state of the art for adapter-based TSFM transfer under a strictly frozen backbone: $54/54$ wins on the primary backbone ($26$--$79\%$ MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter), strictly beats LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning at $p{<}0.001$, and generalizes across four further backbones and an imputation task. Eight causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and reveal a \emph{Frozen Paradox}: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$. Applying the same raw-signal principle at the expert level (Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}) closes the gap with DLinear on $107/123$ cells across six backbones $\times$ four horizons.
59	\end{abstract}
```

1	Pioneering time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT, TimesFM, and TTM pair a frozen backbone with a task-specific head. Because downstream series mix heterogeneous regimes, the natural upgrade is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) head: a router over architecturally distinct experts, one per window. However, this upgrade fails on instance-normalized backbones: Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN) and similar layers strip the per-window mean and variance the router needs, a failure we call *normalization-induced routing collapse*. Whenever joint training makes even one backbone transformer block trainable, routing entropy falls to $0.000$, meaning that every input is sent to a single expert and the rest are never activated. A 720-run sweep across standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, expert-choice) recovers at most $10.9\%$ MSE ($2.7\times$ worse than our causal intervention), because the failure is in the router's input, not its optimization. We formalize this via a mutual-information decomposition; the resulting signal-ratio statistic, computable before training, predicts dataset vulnerability (Spearman $\rho = -0.88$, $p < 0.002$) and flags boundary cases. The diagnosis prescribes a minimal causal intervention: *Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters* (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA sets a new SOTA for adapter-based TSFM transfer under a strictly frozen backbone: 54/54 wins on the primary backbone (26–79% MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter), strictly beats LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning at $p < 0.001$, and generalizes across four further backbones and an imputation task. Eight causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and predict a *Frozen Paradox*: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by 12–79%, dominated by the MoE pool's architectural advantage with a smaller within-architecture freeze effect ($\leq 13\%$) attributable to gradient co-adaptation (Proposition 1). Applying the same raw-signal principle at the expert level (Residual-IA$^+$) closes the gap with DLinear on 107/123 cells across six backbones $\times$ four horizons.
2

320:\node[font=\tiny, text=black] at (12.5, 0.2) {weighted};
485:\emph{(M2) Within-architecture freeze effect (smaller, $\leq 13\%$, non-unidirectional):} within RR-MoA itself, frozen is best on $4/6$ datasets; light unfreezing wins by up to $13\%$ on the other two; SR-MoA independently replicates the $4/6$ pattern (Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen} attributes M2 to gradient co-adaptation: the Router-Detached Gradient Flow ablation (unfreezes backbone but cuts routing-weighted feedback) degrades MSE by $+35$--$100\%$ on three datasets (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}), and disabling RevIN inside MOMENT recovers full-FT by $54$--$61\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}), pinning the mechanism on normalization rather than optimization. A $90$-config hyperparameter tune (LR $10^{-5}$--$10^{-6}$, $100$ epochs, cosine + warmup, layerwise decay, batch up to $512$) still loses by $51$--$71\%$, consistent with optimization not being the bottleneck within the sweep we ran.}
1323:    at ([yshift=-0.05cm]sum.south) {weighted sum};
1481:\textbf{Router-Detached Gradient Flow (RDGF).} A variant that unfreezes the backbone but replaces routing-weighted gradients with uniform-average gradients ($\nabla_H \mathcal{L}_\text{backbone} = \frac{1}{K}\sum_k \nabla_H \mathcal{L}_k$) degrades MSE by $+35\%$ to $+100\%$ across the three datasets we tested (ETTh1, ETTm1, Weather; frozen MOMENT-small last-2, 3 seeds; per-run JSONs released on acceptance). This is consistent with the Frozen Paradox being structural rather than an artifact of full-gradient unfreezing.
1933:\caption{\textbf{Backbone features vs.\ raw input} (H=96, 80/20 train/test split). k-NN: k-nearest neighbor regression ($k{=}20$, distance-weighted) on mean-pooled backbone features vs.\ raw 512-dim input. Ridge: regularized linear regression (cross-validated $\alpha$). Backbone features are $16$--$33\%$ worse than raw input even with a universal approximator (k-NN), confirming genuine information destruction.}
2276:\node[font=\tiny, text=black, anchor=north] at (13.5, -0.45) {weighted sum};
